#506. Best Pet Camera of 2026: Furbo, Petcube, and Enabot

The best pet camera for you depends on what you value for your pet. Personally, I never knew I needed a pet camera, but after trying several models, I don’t think I can go into the office or on vacation without one set up at home. A home security camera can give you extra peace of mind when you’re out, and pet cameras specifically designed for our furry friends add helpful features, like the ability to follow your pet around, engage in play, and dispense kibble. Connected to an app on your phone and with cloud storage options, these pet cameras—including our top pick for most people (and pets!), the Furbo Mini 360° ($49), and our compact 360 rotating budget pick, Petcube Cam 360 ($47)—ensure you’ll never miss a moment in your pet’s life when you’re away (or at home).

For more peace of mind while you’re away from your pet, be sure to check out our related guides, like the Best Automatic Feeders, Best Cat Water Fountains, Best Automatic Litter Boxes, Best Indoor Security Cameras, and Best Outdoor Security Cameras.

Updated October 2026: I’ve added the SwitchBot Pan/Tilt Cam Plus 3K and Amazon Blink Mini 2K+ to Others Tested, and retested the Enabot Ebo Max FamilyBot. I’ve also ensured up-to-date links and prices throughout.

Although it's a great pet camera overall, this model shares a pitfall that affects the rest of the brand’s lineup: Its features are seriously limited unless you pay for Petcube’s upgraded Care plan (which starts at $4 a month), which includes video storage, pet detection, and automatic video recording. Even so, this camera has 360 PTZ rotation, 1080p HD resolution, 8x digital zoom, two-way audio, and night vision. Getting all of these included bells and whistles for around $50 makes this camera a super-affordable buy.

Optional mounting requires some tools, which can make setup tricky. Once up and running, the camera feed rotated smoothly, and with the wide, fisheye-like lens and pan-and-tilt capabilities (including up and down), I could clearly see more of the room than I thought possible. It covered more space than most pet cameras I’ve tested. Plus, with the new privacy mode, you can easily turn off the camera lens for added security. I was pleasantly surprised by this 360 PTZ camera and its seriously affordable price.

This dual camera features 3K high definition, with a 360-degree rotating top camera and a stationary camera below for even more coverage. There’s also live view, two-way chat, night vision, and 6X zoom. AI detection alerts you to events like barks, meows, loud noises, and fire—really anything alarming. Through the app, you can view live footage, review footage stored on the microSD card or in the cloud, and share the content with family. Plus, you can access smart voice control with Amazon Alexa or Google Assistant.

There's an adjustable mount you can install on the ceiling for even greater range. It provides some of the clearest images I’ve seen during testing, and I love that the dual views let me see what my pets are doing on the ground and the 360-degree views above at the same time.

Using pan rotation for an almost 360-degree viewing angle, this model also has a crystal-clear 2K HD live video feed and intelligent infrared night vision. The bottom of the camera has suction cups, making it easy to move around the house without hardware. Through the app, you can pan the camera, take photos, and record video, talk and listen to your pet through the microphone, schedule regular feedings, or toss your pet a snack. This camera has motion, bark, and voice detection, along with AI auto-tracking. Essentially, when the camera detects something out of the norm, the app sends you a notification.

Its AI features create a minute-long video from motion-triggered recordings and automatically save it for 30 days. From there, you can download and share it on socials. You can opt for cloud storage (with the ability to upgrade to a monthly subscription for 30 days of continuous recording) or buy a memory card (up to 128 GB) for 24/7 recording. The video is always hi-def and clear, never laggy or showing error messages. I also love that I can pan to see the whole room.

The Furbo 360 is marketed toward dogs, but both cats and dogs will like its treat-dispensing feature. (Read our full review here.) Very similar to the Tkenpro 2K Pet Camera Treat Dispenser pick, the major selling points of this Furbo are its rotation and kibble dispensing.

The pet camera also has a crystal-clear 1080p color night-vision camera, two-way communication, noise notifications, and 360-degree pan capabilities. It also uses auto-tracking to follow your pet, making it easier to find them on your phone screen while using the app. Just remember, you'll need to pay for Furbo’s Nanny service for features like alerts and video playback storage.

A rarity for pet camera services, this pet camera promises all the best features without a required subscription. It has three lenses, with 360-degree horizontal and 270-degree vertical views, two-way audio, and clear 8-MP color and night vision. When you set up the camera, you'll create pet profiles for each pet, and the camera will learn to track and identify each pet independently. Through the app, you can view footage categorized as daily, weekly, and monthly, by pet and by activity type (like vocalizations and abnormalities). Unlike most other AI-enabled pet products (including our Petlibro camera pick below), the AI correctly identified and categorized my cats during my testing. The pet camera has many customizable features, including sleep mode, scan settings, a parked position, and the ability to share footage with up to five people.

This camera is ready to use out of the box, with 32 GB of storage and AI features included at no extra cost. If you want extras, there's the extended cloud storage plan, which starts at $6 per month and also includes additional replays and downloads.

In my opinion, anything beyond what’s included isn’t super necessary. The only drawback is the sometimes chaotic camera whirring and auto-tracking in the app. Sometimes auto-tracking would pan right past my cats and land on a nearby fan. I’d have to wait for the auto-tracking cycle to complete, then manually reset.

I found that this rolling pet camera is the most polished of all the Enabot wheeled pet camera models I’ve tested. It delivers clear 2K video and black-and-white night vision. It weighs just over a pound, works on 2.4 and 5 GHz Wi-Fi, climbs slopes up to 15 degrees, and can clear obstacles up to 10 millimeters. It charges via a magnetic cord, and the battery lasts up to 25 days, so unlike other models, you don’t need to charge it frequently. The camera sleeps when it’s not in use to conserve battery.

You can use the app to virtually control camera speed and movement, as well as action and sound settings that spin, zigzag, and swing the device and send meows, purrs, and duck quacks. You decide whether each action or sound plays once or for three minutes. The app also lets you take pictures or video recordings. The camera has a built-in 32 GB microSD card, but you can opt for a cloud storage subscription.

You can schedule recordings, and the camera also has automatic motion detection, recording 10-second videos when it detects humans or pets. Additional privacy features include camera control, allowing you to disable the in-app camera view. You can also manage individual user permissions and controls, such as customizing device-sharing schedules and in-app notifications when someone views or records footage. Although my cats are still scared of and perplexed by the animal noises and erratic movements, this camera has a surprisingly wide field of view, a solid ability to cover my home, an always-clear video feed, and a super-long battery life.

The newest—and only—camera model from one of my favorite pet tech brands, Petlibro, is sleek, and the app is intuitive and easy to use. (Read my full review here.) The camera offers 1080p HD video, black-and-white and color night vision (you can choose your preference), two-way audio, and a mounting option. It works with both 2.4 and 5 GHz Wi-Fi networks, and you just need to set up profiles for your pets in the app to get started. As the Scout camera captures footage, the built-in AI (which requires an additional subscription plan that starts at $12 per month) begins to learn which pet is which, uploading photos for the human to review so the AI continually gets smarter. Throughout testing, the camera continuously asked me to categorize pets to ensure each was labeled correctly, and through the app, you could view historical footage by activity type and cat that the AI sorted.

Through the app, you can view live footage, watch clips organized by day, pet, and activity type, and review 30-second highlight reels for each day. You can also easily send feedback on each pet and behavior with a click of a button, so the AI can learn to label them correctly. Overall, I personally don't think the upgraded built-in AI features are worth the subscription price for me, but it’s still a techy pet camera worth checking out.

There’s a lot to love about this compact pet camera on wheels. It uses a 16 GB SD card (which you can swap for a larger one if needed) instead of a pricier cloud subscription, and it handles varied terrain easily (up to 4 degrees uphill), from high-pile carpet to hardwood to doorways. It also has a charging dock, so it doesn't need to be plugged in manually. Maintenance is super easy: When the pet camera-robot’s battery runs low, it automatically returns to the charging dock using built-in infrared scanning technology. Charging takes about two and a half hours and lasts for about five hours.

The pet camera on wheels works on both 2.4 and 5 GHz Wi-Fi. Through the built-in app, you can adjust movement speed, control the camera's movements; use voice chat to talk to and listen to your pets; take photos or videos; and “play,” which refers to action movements like moving in circles and swinging. You’ll also receive app notifications for events like unusual robot movement (such as being stuck on carpet or in a corner) and low battery. You can store and review footage and photos through the app. While it doesn't have as many features as the Enabot Rola Mini (who really needs their pet camera to make duck noises?), it’s a solid smart camera that can surveil your entire house for a little less money.

Like the dual-lens pet camera pick above, this is one of the cameras I’ve tested that covers the most area. The 2K camera on the bottom stays fixed, while the panning camera above pans left/right and up/down for nearly 360-degree views of the camera’s surroundings. It also comes with an optional mount if you’d rather hang it from a ceiling for even more bird's-eye coverage. (I live in an apartment, so I set it upright on various surfaces in my house throughout testing.) The front has a call button to call a pre-saved contact with one press, and the pet camera stores historical video feed on a microSD card (not included) rather than a pricey cloud storage system. I appreciate this because I much prefer storing video on a disk rather than through a company’s internal cloud storage, which can be unsafe and has recurring costs that add up.

Through the connected Lorex app, you can view live footage (and historical footage with a memory card), pan the camera, take photos and videos, talk to and hear from your pet, and adjust settings such as volume and video resolution. With both cameras, you can adjust each camera's settings independently, including tracking and detection sensitivity, and enable AI auto-tracking for pets and humans. Online reviewers had trouble with an unreliable Wi-Fi connection, which led to a laggy feed. Others complained that the camera's panning abilities caused the app to freeze, which could be resolved only by manually rebooting the camera. However, I did not encounter either issue during my weeks-long testing.

Enabot Ebo Max FamilyBot for $600: I’m usually a big fan of Enabot’s pet tech—especially its line of pet cameras, which are generally affordable and work consistently well without needing a pricey add-on cloud storage system. This camera is touted as an AI family companion that can recognize pets and household members, remember routines and preferences, and come when called. It also has the expected pet camera-on-wheels features, like two-way audio and video, night vision, and 4K imaging. During initial testing, the camera didn't charge properly on the dock and often froze or stopped charging. I got a second model to test, and it worked better—it handled tasks on command, returned to the charging station, and recognized my cats. However, I still don't think its AI-powered companion features are cool enough to justify that price tag. If price is no issue for you and you want a moving camera on wheels, it's worth checking out.

SwitchBot Pan/Tilt Cam Plus 3K for $50: I generally liked this pan/tilt camera, which offers a 360-degree horizontal field of view and a 115-degree vertical field of view, and can be mounted with the provided tools. It also has two-way audio, voice commands, and smart-home support like Amazon Alexa or Google Home. It has eight infrared LEDs that support enhanced night vision, with a range of up to 32 feet. Through the app, you can adjust camera settings for motion detection or tracking and switch to privacy mode when you want the camera to stop recording. However, it only works with 2.4-GHz Wi-Fi and, like many other cams on this list, requires a paid subscription plan to access enhanced features like AI recognition, smart event summaries, unlimited cloud storage, and access to historical recordings. It also required several firmware updates throughout testing and would freeze during updates. It also lagged when moving through the app and often froze for a prolonged period when first opening the app.

Amazon Blink Mini 2K+ for $40: This small, budget-friendly camera supports 2K video, 4X hybrid zoom, and color night vision. In his testing, reviewer Simon Hill found its motion detection reliable, but only with a subscription: $3 per month or $30 per year for one camera, or $10 per month or $100 per year for unlimited cameras. You can record locally by purchasing a Sync Module 2 ($50) and inserting a USB flash drive. Luckily, you don't need a subscription to record the motion-triggered footage from the camera to the Sync Module 2. You just need to insert an approved USB flash drive (up to 256 GB) into the Sync Module 2 to enable subscription-free local storage. Without a subscription, however, live viewing is limited to five minutes, recording is unavailable, videos load much more slowly, people/animal detection isn’t included, and you can’t share clips.

Enabot Ebo Air 2 for $199: The new model from Enabot is an improved interactive robot toy and camera on wheels from the brand’s lineup. It’s got a 2K HD camera, two-way audio, and a built-in laser toy, and you control the robot’s movements from your phone’s app. It’s lightweight and compact, and it can easily maneuver around objects. While acting as a pet camera, the robot also entertains (or scares or confuses) your pet by making animal noises, doing tricks like spinning and zigzagging, and displaying “emotions” via its front screen. It also has a built-in laser toy that my cats actually played with, along with key features like 24/7 video recording with smart playback, motion detection, and activity alerts.

Petcube Play 2 for $75: This Petcube camera attempts to combine popular laser toys that cats love with a pet security cam, with mixed results. Like many other cameras in the brand’s lineup, the Play 2 has excellent color and night vision, an extra-wide view, and two-way audio. The laser toy is supposed to auto-engage when motion is detected, but this often didn’t happen during my tests. When the laser did work and caught my cats’ attention, the feature turned off quickly. Plus, because the camera needed to sit high on a shelf to see the area, the laser was often too high for the cats to attempt to catch. You can manually play with the cat using the laser through the app, although it’s often laggy, and my cats ended up more perplexed than playful when the laser was on. It’s a solid camera, but the toy aspect falls a bit flat.

Furbo Mini for $25: I love this super-affordable, 360-degree-rotating basic pet camera. Furbo’s app is easy to use, the 1080 HD feed is clear, it can be mounted on a wall, and it has two-way audio. It’s powered via USB, but it works only with 2.4 GHz Wi-Fi, so beware. As with most other pet cameras, to access the full features—like smart detection, alerts, and access to historical video footage—you’ll need Furbo Nanny, the paid subscription service. Luckily, Furbo offers a 14-day free trial to see if the service is a good fit.

Petcube Bites 2 Lite for $80: With HD 1080p live video, night vision up to 30 feet, 8x zoom, two-way audio so you can talk to and hear your pets, and food-dispensing abilities, this pet camera has all of the bells and whistles. It also includes an easily detachable plastic treat dispenser that holds up to 1.5 pounds of kibble. You can toss treats remotely from the app, and you can also control the amount and distance. With the subscription service, the app alerts you when the device detects motion or sound and logs it with playback video. I like the customization options and clear video feed, but—like other Petcube devices—you need the extra Care plan to unlock all the best features.

Enabot Rola PetPal Robot Pet Companion for $200 (treat dispensing): A pet camera on wheels, this rolling model has the best of intentions, but the sometimes glitchy app makes the combo pet camera/toy/feeder a little unpredictable. Through the app, you can watch your pets on the security camera and drive the robot around, pressing buttons to do tricks and dispense treats. You can watch the camera feed live in the app, and you can also schedule recordings, which are viewable via cloud playback. It also assembles a 30-second “day in the life” compilation. The robot is also supposed to detect abnormal movement or sounds, like barking or broken glass, and send a smart alert to the owner with a timestamp while away from home. Through AI-powered pet recognition, the bot can also snap a picture of your pet, but the AI features were unreliable when I tested them.

Petcube Cam for $32: It's simple, compact, and doesn't have all the bells and whistles like more advanced models do, but it’s a great price for a reliable, basic pet camera. Like others in Petcube's lineup, it has a 1080p HD camera, night vision, and two-way audio. It sits on hinges and can angle up and down, with the option to flip the video feed in-app if you want to lay it horizontally. You'll need the brand's subscription Care plan to access any features beyond the live feed. You can set a “motion zone” to receive notifications only in a specific area, and you can connect your phone’s location to the app so you’re alerted more often when you’re away. You can also share the camera feed with others so they can watch remotely, and specify the footage you want to share. This camera is basic, but it delivers a high-quality camera feed and advanced extras that make it a steal at this price.

Catit Pixi Smart Mouse Camera for $51: This cutie looks like a miniature mouse with oversized, magnetic feet that attach to a metal stand. The mouse legs hinge so you can adjust the camera’s height and angle. While adorable, it’s expensive for what it is: The lens doesn't move, and it requires a microSD card to capture video beyond a live feed. With the SD card, you can scroll through recorded video to see activity from past days, record live footage, and save photos and videos to your smartphone’s camera roll. Even with the SD card, though, it was hard to pinpoint the exact time when reviewing footage. The live video feed can be laggy, and there were connectivity issues a few times. It’s a tad expensive at this price point for its (lack of) capabilities.

Closer Pets TreatView with Pet Camera for $100: With an HD camera, two-way communication, motion detection, a built-in memory card, and a one-cup storage for kibble or treats, which you can schedule or dispense at random—on paper, this pet camera has all of the bells and whistles. The video feed was always clear, though the audio was a bit laggy and hard to hear. The treat portions were huge, dispensing a heaping handful of kibble even at the lowest setting. The dispensing alert was super jarring, and my pets were frightened by it—there was no way to silence it. Because of the kibble-dispensing hole's placement, the camera is angled low, making it hard to see the area I wanted. This is an okay pet camera, but its additional features become a burden rather than an asset.

To find the best pet cameras to test, I first looked for security cameras with extra pet-specific features. I set up each camera individually, noting any difficulties with initial setup, Wi-Fi configuration, and the camera feed's speed and reliability. I tested each camera for at least a week (most for much longer) in various spots around my house, and I rigorously tested the extra features available on most of the cameras, like scheduled feedings, food-dispensing, playtime, and scheduled recordings, noting any issues I experienced. Over the months of testing, I went out of town several times and completely relied on these cams to keep an eye on my two children—I mean, cats.

So, Is There Really a Difference Between Security Cameras and Pet Cameras? Sort Of

Security cameras let you keep an eye on your house (and pet) while you’re away, but pet cameras often include additional pet-specific features, such as the ability to dispense treats and interact with your furry friend via a two-way speaker. These cameras with special features can help ease common pet issues like separation anxiety or boredom. The Enabot Rola PetPal Robot Pet Companion, for example, has attachments that let you remotely give your pet a treat or engage in “playtime.”

That's not to say you can’t use a security camera as a pet cam, but like any other smart home device that connects to Wi-Fi, it could be hacked and compromise your privacy. Make sure your Wi-Fi password is strong, and set up two-factor authentication in your camera’s app. It’s also a good idea to keep it turned off when you’re home, as an extra precaution.

#505. HSBC's top execs face tense shareholders calling for a breakup

HSBC’s top brass defended their strategy Monday to frustrated shareholders in the lender’s largest market, as Europe’s biggest bank continued to face calls to be split up.

At an informal shareholder meeting in Hong Kong, Chairman Mark Tucker and CEO Noel Quinn took questions from investors on issues ranging from how the bank was approaching demands for an overhaul of its business to its purchase of Silicon Valley Bank’s UK arm.

In prepared remarks, Tucker and Quinn each reiterated the board’s recommendation that shareholders vote against a resolution on the docket for its annual general meeting in May that would force the bank to come up with a plan to spin off or reorganize its Asian business — the lender’s main source of profits.

HSBC's biggest shareholder reignites debate about breaking up the bank

Tucker said the board was unanimous in its opposition to the resolution, stating plainly: “It would not be in your interest to split the bank.”

He said the board had previously reviewed a range of options for restructuring the bank, and concluded that such alternatives would “materially destroy value for shareholders,” including dividends.

“Our strategy is working,” Tucker told the room of more than 1,000 shareholders. “Our current strategy is moving dividends up.”

HSBC has been facing calls to separate its Asian business from the rest of the bank over the past year.

Shareholders in Hong Kong — where HSBC is a mainstay of many retail investors’ portfolios — contend that the London-based lender’s performance has been dragged down by its businesses in other regions.

Quinn addressed those complaints head-on Monday, saying “our profits in Hong Kong and the UK are no longer being dragged down by underperformance elsewhere. The group is performing well as a whole.”

Pressed later by a shareholder on the issue, Quinn said a breakup of the bank would result in “significant revenue loss” because much of its business relied on cross-border transactions.

Investors have also been unhappy with HSBC scrapping its dividend in 2020, at the request of British regulators. They argue that if the lender cordoned off its activities in Asia, it would no longer have to expose Hong Kong shareholders to requests in other jurisdictions.

Christine Fong, a district council member in Hong Kong, said she represented about 500 small shareholders who had been affected by the dividend cancellation.

“Street hawkers, taxi drivers or teachers — they all relied on the dividend to pay for their regular expenses, like mortgage, insurance payments, school fees,” Fong told CNN.

“That’s why, three years ago, what HSBC did upset those small minority shareholders.”

Fong has now joined calls for shareholders to vote in favor of the proposal for the bank to spin off its Asian business, despite the lender bringing back its dividend in 2021, albeit at a lower level.

Ken Lui, an activist shareholder in Hong Kong who put the resolution together, doubled down on his call for support ahead of the meeting Monday.

The resolution will require 75% of votes to be passed in May, but “nothing is impossible,” he told reporters outside the meeting venue.

Lui, who said he personally held a stake worth 100 million Hong Kong dollars ($12.7 million), laid out plans for his team to focus on “targeted outreach to institutional shareholders to present our case and gain their support.”

His group will also canvass 18 districts of Hong Kong “to tell HSBC shareholders that they finally have a chance to speak for themselves and protect their rights through voting,” he added.

HSBC is also facing pressure from its largest shareholder.

Ping An (PNGAY), China’s biggest insurer, holds an 8% stake in HSBC and has backed calls for the bank to rethink its structure.

In a series of remarks made public by the Chinese firm last November, Huang Yong, chairman of Ping An’s asset management arm, said “we will support any initiatives including a spinoff that are conducive to improve HSBC’s performance and value.”

Since then, the insurance giant’s views haven’t changed, according to a person familiar with the matter.

The source told CNN that Ping An has been calling for HSBC to explore a reorganization, with an eye on boosting its valuation and simplifying its regulatory obligations around the globe.

The insurer has not recommended a specific path forward but will support any initiatives, including a spinoff of its Asian business, that could boost its stock performance or value, the person added. Ping An did not immediately respond to a request for comment on how it planned to vote at the upcoming general meeting.

HSBC’s leaders were also asked Monfday why the bank had scooped up the British unit of SVB following the stunning collapse of its parent in the United States. The purchase was made for £1 ($1.20) last month, just days after SVB had folded.

Critics have questioned HSBC’s ability to perform adequate due diligence on SVB UK’s customers because of how quickly the deal came together.

“Did HSBC look into the clients of SVB in detail? Say, the financial statement — whether they can pay back the loan?” said Fong.

HSBC buys SVB's UK business, ending 'nightmare' for British tech

Quinn and Tucker defended the acquisition, calling it a good business opportunity that allowed the bank to gain hundreds of innovative startups as customers. They pushed back on the notion that management hadn’t had time to carry out proper due diligence.

Tucker also weighed in on recent tumult in the banking industry, saying he did not expect an “immediate impact” on HSBC.

“After the collapse of a number of smaller regional banks and the takeover of Credit Suisse, the share prices of all banks have been suppressed,” he noted.

But he said he did not believe such developments represented “a systemic risk” to the sector. “I do expect a period of uncertainty” before nerves settle, he added.

#503. Three giant black holes discovered in a galaxy from the dawn of time

Astronomers have discovered three actively feeding supermassive black holes in a single galaxy more than 12.5 billion light-years from Earth. The finding, made by an international research team led by the Max Planck Institute for Extraterrestrial Physics (MPE), offers a rare glimpse of how enormous black holes grew and interacted during the universe's earliest stages.

The black holes are located in a galaxy known as J0148-4214. All three are actively accumulating matter, which spirals toward them through surrounding disks of gas and other material. Because the galaxy is so distant, its light has traveled through space for approximately 12.5 billion years before reaching Earth. This means astronomers are observing it as it existed just 1.2 billion years after the Big Bang.

Over such vast distances, the expansion of the universe stretches light to longer, redder wavelengths. Astronomers measure this effect through a property called redshift. For J0148-4214, the researchers measured a redshift of z=5.02, allowing them to determine its remarkable distance.

"This is the first evidence of three active black holes in a single galaxy in the distant Universe," says Hannah Übler, research group leader at MPE and lead author of the study.

Two of the black holes lie near the galaxy's center, separated by a projected distance of just 620 light-years. The third is much farther out, approximately 5,500 light-years from the center.

The discovery provides important evidence about how massive black holes came together in the young universe.

"It suggests that processes in the early Universe were efficient at bringing massive black holes together, setting the stage for the massive black hole mergers we expect to detect with future gravitational wave observatories," says Übler.

Astronomers believe that galaxy collisions and mergers were common during the universe's early history. As galaxies combined, their central black holes could also move closer together and eventually merge. These mergers would produce increasingly massive black holes at the centers of the newly formed galaxies, potentially helping explain how some grew to enormous sizes so soon after the Big Bang.

How Astronomers Detected the Hidden Black Holes

The researchers identified the three black holes by examining distinctive patterns in the light emitted by hydrogen gas. Near a black hole, powerful gravitational forces cause gas to move at extremely high speeds. These movements leave recognizable signatures in the spectrum of light, allowing astronomers to detect black hole activity even when the objects themselves cannot be seen directly.

In the center of J0148-4214, the team found a particularly complicated hydrogen emission pattern. The best explanation was the presence of two closely spaced black holes rather than a single source.

To distinguish between them, the scientists used a precise measurement technique called spectro-astrometry. By tracking subtle changes in the positions of spectral emissions across the galaxy, they were able to identify the locations of both central black holes, even though the telescope could not resolve them as two separate points of light.

The observations also revealed the third active black hole in the galaxy's outer region.

Three Black Holes With Remarkably Different Masses

The three black holes vary dramatically in size. According to the researchers' calculations, their masses are approximately 80 million, 600,000, and 2 million times that of the Sun.

Surprisingly, the largest black hole is not the one feeding most aggressively. Its smaller neighbor, with a mass of roughly 600,000 suns, is accumulating matter at a much higher rate. In fact, its estimated feeding rate exceeds the maximum predicted by basic models of black hole growth (the Eddington limit).

This unusually rapid growth may offer clues to how some black holes became extraordinarily massive during the relatively short time available in the early universe.

"The JWST data allowed us not only to identify the three black holes, but also to estimate their masses, accretion rates, and the stellar mass of the galaxy," says Dr. Giovanni Mazzolari, second author of the study and researcher at MPE. "We find a total stellar mass of about 1.3 billion suns, and the black holes represent a significant fraction of that."

An Ancient Black Hole Merger in the Making

The two black holes near the galaxy's center are expected to merge within the next few hundred million years. Their eventual collision could help produce an even more massive black hole, illustrating a process that may have been essential to the growth of the universe's earliest cosmic giants.

"These results are extremely exciting," adds Roberto Maiolino, professor at the University of Cambridge and co-author of the study. "They suggest that black hole merging may be an additional, fast route for their rapid growth in the early Universe."

The third black hole presents another intriguing puzzle. Located well outside the galaxy's center, it could be a remnant of an earlier merger that was pushed outward by a powerful gravitational recoil. Such a kick can occur when merging black holes emit gravitational waves unevenly, sending the resulting black hole in the opposite direction.

Another possibility is that the third black hole is gradually moving toward the galaxy's center, where it may eventually interact with the other two.

James Webb Reveals Black Holes That Might Otherwise Remain Hidden

The discovery highlights the remarkable capabilities of the James Webb Space Telescope (JWST) for studying black holes in distant galaxies.

In particular, the findings demonstrate the importance of integral field spectroscopy, which allows astronomers to examine how light and its spectral signatures vary across different parts of a galaxy.

Using Webb's Near Infrared Spectrograph in its integral field spectroscopy mode (NIRSpec-IFS), the researchers were able to separate signals that would otherwise have appeared to come from a single black hole.

Without that detailed spatial information, astronomers likely would have identified only one of the galaxy's three active black holes.

The results suggest that other distant galaxies may contain multiple growing black holes that have gone undetected. Finding more of these hidden systems could help scientists reconstruct the history of black hole mergers and better understand how the universe produced its most massive black holes so early in cosmic history.

Materials provided by Max Planck Institute for Extraterrestrial Physics, Garching. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#502. R.J. Mitte’s Mother Shares Tribute After His Death: “We Are Lost Without Him”

The 'Breaking Bad' actor and activist died earlier this month at age 34.

Dyna Mitte is paying tribute to R.J. Mitte 15 days after discovering her son at his Texas home after he took his own life at age 34.

“We are lost without him, yet we know he wouldn’t want us to be,” Dyna wrote about the Breaking Bad actor and activist in a social media post on Tuesday. “He would want his message heard and the work we were doing with The Mitte Foundation in Brownsville to continue.”

In her touching tribute posted to Instagram, Dyna wrote, “What can I say about my precious child? From the day we got RJ at 3 days old, he has been inspiring and sharing his heart with everyone he met. Whether he was learning to manage and later thrive with cerebral palsy or finding his way in the acting community, it required hard work, but he succeeded and was always ready for the next challenge. It became a joy watching his way with people, the way he naturally lit up in every room he entered and instantly charmed his way into people’s lives like they were family.” Related Stories General News R.J. Mitte's Mother Opens Up About His Tragic Passing to Help Other Families (While Clarifying Speculation About His Finances, Career and Fiancée) TV R.J. Mitte Remembered by 'Breaking Bad' Casting Director Sharon Bialy

R.J. Mitte was remembered earlier this month during an Oct. 16 Celebration of Life ceremony at the Mitte Center in Brownsville, Texas, where hundreds filled the seats and an overflow building to share tributes. The family was working on another celebration of life in Los Angeles at the end of the month, Dyna recently said in an interview with The Hollywood Reporter around that time, where she said she was speaking out in hopes of reaching anyone else who might be struggling.

“It’s important for parents to realize that there may be big signs or small signs. If you notice, even in conversation, that there are struggles or questions, be really in tune with your children and take the steps you can to help,” she said. “I thought R.J. was in a place where we had gotten over the hump and that he was OK. It wasn’t until he didn’t answer the phone that I recognized it as a red flag, because we talked regularly.”

She also clarified that her son was doing well in his career, quieting speculation suggesting otherwise. “R.J. had three movies that have already come out this year. He has two more coming out,” she told THR. “He has already signed deals on several new movies that he was going to be filming. There were talks of different things. He had a book coming out. He had a food truck he was starting. He had a lot going on.”

What can I say about my precious child? From the day we got RJ at 3 days old, he has been inspiring and sharing his heart with everyone he met. Whether he was learning to manage and later thrive with cerebral palsy or finding his way in the acting community, it required hard work, but he succeeded and was always ready for the next challenge. It became a joy watching his way with people, the way he naturally lit up in every room he entered and instantly charmed his way into people’s lives like they were family.

Life with RJ was a constant adventure. The places we traveled for his acting and appearances, though for work, he always made them fun. From Florida to Lithuania, he knew all the historical facts and places we should see, and we would always go just to satisfy his neverending curiosity. I can never express how proud I was of everything he overcame to become the man the world was just beginning to know. The years he spent advocating developed a deep empathy for those who needed him to be their voice and it was never a burden, but a role he loved to play. We are lost without him, yet we know he wouldn’t want us to be. He would want his message heard and the work we were doing with The Mitte Foundation in Brownsville to continue.

In time, I will get back to everyone who has reached out and shared their stories about RJ. Please know I’m seeing them and appreciate how much he meant to you all. My son was an incredibly special and sensitive soul who did so much for his family, his community, and the world, and we will mourn him for the rest of our days.

#501. 'Star Trek' legend and sci-fi luminary Walter Koenig dies at 90

The man who portrayed the U.S.S. Enterprise’s Pavel Chekov has left us after a 60-year stage and screen career

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The "Star Trek" community lost one of its most beloved icons on Sunday, October 25, with the sad passing of Walter Koenig at the age of 90 in his North Hollywood home.

Koenig was best known as the U.S.S. Enterprise's Russian navigator Pavel Andreievich Chekov for two seasons on "Star Trek: The Original Series." The popular American actor continued in that role for Paramount's first seven "Star Trek" films, which began in 1979's "Star Trek: The Motion Picture" and ended with 1994's "Star Trek: Generations."

A native of Chicago, Illinois, Marvin Walter Koenig was born on Sept. 14, 1936, to Lithuanian immigrant parents and spent his formative years in New York City. After a short college stint at UCLA in California, Koenig was accepted into the prestigious Neighborhood Playhouse School of the Theatre in New York in 1958, joining future stars like James Caan and Christopher Lloyd.

TV guest spots in the early '60s on "Mr. Novak" and "Ben Casey" led Koenig to join the intrepid crew of the U.S.S. Enterprise for Gene Roddenberry’s sophomore series of "Star Trek." He co-starred in 36 of the 50 installments airing on the NBC Network in 1967-68 and 1968-69, premiering in famed sci-fi author Theodore Sturgeon's excellent episode, "Amok Time," broadcast on Sept. 15, 1967.

Although Koenig's Ensign Chekov character was not resurrected for the 1973-74 Saturday morning cartoon spinoff, "Star Trek: The Animated Series," due to budget issues, he did write a screenplay for that show’s first season titled "The Infinite Vulcan."

In 1982's "Star Trek II: The Wrath of Khan," he was promoted to Commander aboard the U.S.S. Reliant when he and Captain Terrell (Paul Winfield) beamed down to Seti Alpha V and ran into the genetically enhanced Khan Noonien Singh (Ricardo Montalban).

Following his work in the "Star Trek" movies, Koenig scored an ongoing TV role on creator J. Michael Straczynski's "Babylon 5" starting in 1993, where he portrayed the former Psi Ops commander and war criminal, Alfred Bester, through the 1998 season.

A core member of the original "Star Trek" and its Hollywood features alongside William Shatner, Leonard Nimoy, DeForest Kelley, George Takei, Nichelle Nichols, and James Doohan, Koenig was often utilized as comic relief.

His thick accent, where he pronounced his “Vs” as “Ws,” was derived from hearing his Russian-Jewish father's particular manner of speech. Koenig’s undeniable contributions to the legacy of "Star Trek" and the chemistry of its cast cannot be overestimated, and he'll be sorely missed.

His bestselling 1998 autobiography, "Warped Factors: A Neurotic’s Guide to the Universe," was later followed by a detailed memoir titled "Beaming Up and Getting Off," released back in 2020.

Koenig is survived by his sole daughter, screenwriter and actress Danielle Koenig.

Jeff Spry is an award-winning screenwriter and veteran freelance journalist covering TV, movies, video games, books, and comics. His work has appeared at SYFY Wire, Inverse, Collider, Bleeding Cool and elsewhere. Jeff lives in beautiful Bend, Oregon amid the ponderosa pines, classic muscle cars, a crypt of collector horror comics, and two loyal English Setters.

SAVE $40: As of Oct. 27, Apple AirPods Pro 3 are on sale for $199.99 at Amazon. That's a 20% discount on the list price.

Apple's AirPods Pro 3 have dipped below $200 at Amazon, so if you've been eyeing up the premium earbuds for early Christmas shopping or an upgrade for yourself, now is a good time to buy. Normally $249.99, this new deal drops the price to $199.99, saving you $50.

The AirPods Pro 3 are a great choice if noise cancellation is important to you. They block up to twice as much unwanted background noise as the previous generation, and the new acoustic system also delivers improvements to bass, clarity, and detail.

Built-in heart rate monitoring is incredibly clever, letting you track your pulse and estimated calorie burn during workouts without needing a separate fitness tracker. Using tiny pulses of light to measure blood flow in your ear, the AirPods can record your heart rate throughout a session and send the information to your iPhone.

The battery life is just as impressive. You can get up to eight hours of listening with active noise cancellation enabled, or up to 10 hours in Transparency mode when using the Hearing Aid feature.

#498. Goldman Sachs, JPMorgan and Morgan Stanley are dominating the AI stock boom. Here's their edge

Fueled by the global boom in artificial intelligence stocks, relaxed capital rules and greater lending to hedge funds, big Wall Street banks have already had a banner year for equities trading — even with one quarter left to go.

The five biggest investment banks have generated about $67 billion in equities revenue through Sept. 30, according to company filings. That is $7 billion more than what the group made during all of 2025, which itself was a record haul.

But it's Wall Street's Big Three that have captured the largest slice of what is shaping up to be the industry's most lucrative arena: Goldman Sachs, JPMorgan Chase and Morgan Stanley have made a combined $52 billion in stock trading revenue so far this year, nearly 80% of the total.

"When it comes to the AI mega trade, the Big Three consider each other their main competitors, and that's pretty much it," Mike Mayo, Wells Fargo banking analyst, told CNBC. "They've been best positioned to take advantage of this market."

Wall Street banks are experiencing an epic run of blockbuster profits as the total value of the stock market balloons and trading activity from institutional and retail investors rises across stocks, exchange-traded funds and derivatives. Helping drive this activity are the loans that the banks, through their prime brokerages, are extending to hedge funds so that they can amplify their bets.

Prime brokerage — the business of providing hedge funds with margin loans, securities lending and other trading services — has become a key battleground and source of growth. The Big Three's prime brokers are central to the larger equities story because they serve the largest funds across markets and products.

Demand for leverage to make bets on AI hardware, hyperscalers and chip suppliers has reached new heights, bank executives said last week.

At Goldman Sachs, equities financing activities jumped 96% in the third quarter to a record $3.37 billion. JPMorgan and Morgan Stanley don't break out that figure, but said they saw a similar dynamic.

"I like your 'insatiable demand for capital' point. That feels about right," JPMorgan CFO Jeremy Barnum said on the bank's third-quarter conference call, responding to analyst questions about financing demands.

"In this moment where AI is such an overwhelming theme ... there is capital being tapped through all kinds of different mechanisms," Barnum said.

That demand has helped transform equity desks at the Big Three from cyclical, transactional businesses into more durable, high-margin lending engines, Wall Street executives say.

The performance gap between Wall Street's giants and rivals including Bank of America and Citigroup highlights a widening divide in capital markets.

"For clients, the value isn't just a price on one trade, it's coverage and continuity," Rachid Alaoui, JPMorgan global head of equities, told CNBC in an interview. "You want a dealer that can give you the full suite across products, and you want that service to travel with you across regions. That matters in stressed markets and periods of volatility."

Executing multibillion-dollar bets, like leveraged swaps on volatile AI stocks or sector ETFs, can require an enormous balance sheet. The biggest banks can absorb potential $100 million losses without threatening their quarters, according to trading executives. Smaller or more niche banks can't underwrite single-trade risks of that magnitude, forcing them to decline the business or cede share.

U.S. prime brokers have also benefited from the retreat of European banks including Credit Suisse from the equities business, said Bloomberg Intelligence analyst Herman Chan.

"Prime is one of the ultimate scale businesses," Chan said. "If you have a strong equities franchise, the hedge funds will want to transact with you because you have a bigger pool of trading opportunities with counterparties."

While U.S. markets were swept up in software and cloud computing giants this year, trading executives say the fastest-growing frontier for equities was in Asia.

Institutional investors poured into South Korea and Taiwan, targeting the "picks and shovels" of the AI supply chain: the memory chipmakers and semiconductor manufacturers supporting data center construction, Alaoui said.

For the Big Three, with their footprints across Tokyo; Hong Kong; Seoul, South Korea; and Mumbai, India; the Asia trade was a goldmine. However, trading executives point out that transaction taxes and stamp duties are included in top-line revenue, artificially inflating Asia prime totals.

There is also no agreed-upon definition of what equities trading revenue should include, so some banks include business lines like clearing services that others exclude from their stock trading totals, making apples-to-apples comparisons difficult.

But that doesn't change the overall momentum of the business, boosted by a stock rally that began in October 2022, weeks before OpenAI released ChatGPT.

"We're still in a stock-based world," Morgan Stanley CEO Ted Pick said on the firm's earnings call. "Equities are still, all things being equal, the attractive asset class."

While the CEOs of Goldman Sachs and Morgan Stanley have said the AI build-out is still in its early years, if AI turns out to be not as transformative as hoped, a downturn could impact those banks more than others, analysts said.

The boom has also drawn some concern over the possible risks posed by hedge funds.

The International Monetary Fund warned this month that growing leverage, crowded positions and correlated selling from hedge funds could create potential stability risks. The IMF estimated that global hedge fund assets had reached $13 trillion.

In August, JPMorgan CEO Jamie Dimon said industrywide leverage was "the highest it's ever been," though he stopped short of calling the risk big enough to pose a threat to the financial markets.

Wall Street executives and analysts pointed out that the industry wasn't fazed by the meltdown of several hedge funds this summer. While the hedge fund run by former OpenAI researcher Leopold Aschenbrenner, Situational Awareness, garnered the most headlines, several smaller firms also experienced a forced unwinding.

Prime brokers have learned to leap on margin calls and quickly take down risk when markets move against clients, JPMorgan's Alaoui said.

"In the summer, some investors had positions they were forced to unwind," he told CNBC. "They deleveraged, risk came down, and the market moved on."

Another factor driving the equities boom: Trump administration regulators have eased some leverage rules for the largest U.S. banks, giving them more room to expand balance sheet-heavy businesses including prime brokerage. The revised rules were finalized in 2025, and banks were allowed to adopt them as early as January.

By easing the stringent balance sheet caps that previously treated Treasurys and prime brokerage loans, which are collateralized, the same as riskier assets, regulators gave dealers greater flexibility to deploy balance sheet capital.

"Capital did go down, so you have to say the system is somewhat more levered because of the relaxation in regulations," said Darrell Duffie, a finance professor at Stanford University and a leading expert on financial stability. "It's not a good idea, but it hasn't gotten dramatically out of whack."

The larger size of the market and higher volatility can create more trading, derivatives activity and leverage, he said, but that's not inherently alarming.

Wall Street executives also downplayed concerns over leverage, pointing out that they use collateral and frequent stress testing to get ahead of problems.

"Hedge fund leverage as a contributor of risk to the system is very much in the category of known unknowns," JPMorgan's Barnum said last week. "It doesn't mean it's riskless, but we kind of know what it is."

— CNBC's Leslie Picker and Ritika Shah contributed to this report.

#497. New EU plan for Arctic warily eyes influence of Russia, US

The EU unveiled its priorities in the Arctic as it vies with Russia, China and the US for influence. EU foreign affairs chief Kaja Kallas said drone incursions, GPS jamming and cable sabotage undermine Arctic security.

The EU on Tuesday presented a new Arctic Strategy that outlines the bloc's priorities in the key region amid challenges such as security threats, climate change and mining.

Russia is building up its military power in the Arctic, and US President Donald Trump has threatened Greenland, saying the Danish territory is needed for American security interests but later signed a deal with Denmark over US military access to the territory. China is also investing in the region and has shipping ambitions there.

The northern parts of Finland and Sweden, along with Greenland are located within the Arctic, meaning part of the region is in the European neighborhood.  Iceland and Norway, two other Arctic states, are also involved in the European single market.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

"The Arctic is one place where the tectonic plates of geopolitics are colliding," European Commission President Ursula von der Leyen said.

"When we see that sovereignty, territorial integrity and the rules that have underpinned security in the region can no longer be taken for granted, we have to act and change our strategic posture," she added.

EU High Representative for Foreign Affairs and Security Policy Kaja Kallas said Russia "has reopened Soviet-era bases and is testing new weapons" in the Arctic.

"Drone incursions, GPS jamming and the sabotage of undersea cables undermine Arctic security," Kallas continued. Russia's shadow fleet, which is an attempt to circumvent Western sanctions, is active in the Arctic.

On Arctic security, the EU plans to develop a maritime security hub in the region "to facilitate information sharing form seabed to space." The bloc also intends to expand European icebreaker capacity and extend its satellite surveillance program in the region.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

However, the EU does not intend to compete with NATO in the region, EU Commissioner for International Partnerships Jozef Sikela told DW. Twenty-three EU countries are members of NATO.

"NATO will remain basically the center of defense and the center of deterrence, but NATO doesn't have a budget, the member state has," Sikela said. "This is first about the proper focus and the proper use of resources of the NATO member states and about better coordination."

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

In regard to the climate, the Arctic is warming about four times faster than the global average. The melting of ice and glaciers in the Arctic pose risks to Europe such as prolonged heatwaves, drought and flooding.

Sikela said, "Collaboration among the Arctic is very difficult," adding, "We're not getting information about the permafrost from the biggest part of the Arctic territory."

Sikela accused Russia of "unprecedented" actions in the Arctic and said the "catastrophic level of drilling and opening new mines" in the region is contributing to its rapid warming. The Arctic is rich in critical minerals and rare earth elements such as nickel.

As part of the EU Arctic Strategy, the bloc intends "to strengthen climate research, observation and monitoring" and launch flagship projects utilizing AI to "anticipate extreme weather and cascading disasters such as coastal flooding."

Although Russia and other non-EU countries such as Norway drill in the Arctic, the EU does not want to expand new drilling in the region. European Commissioner for Energy and Housing Dan Jorgensen said the EU's view "on drilling in the Arctic remains the same and it's very clear: oil, coal and gas must stay in the ground."

Regarding economic interests in the Arctic, the bloc may take further steps to bypass the US. The strategy calls for "resilient submarine cable connectivity linking Greenland directly to Europe, with potential future extensions towards Canada and Japan."

The EU also outlined new plans regarding the residents of the Arctic region. The bloc wants to strengthen dialogue with Indigenous people along with expanding Erasmus+ opportunities for Arctic youth.

#496. New mRNA Vaccine Reduces Risk of Melanoma Recurrence by Over 40 Percent

An experimental vaccine offers new hope to patients with melanoma, one of the most aggressive forms of skin cancer. The pharmaceutical companies Merck and Moderna released the results of a Phase 3 clinical trial that evaluated the efficacy of a personalized mRNA-based vaccine that, when combined with immunotherapy, reduced the risk of disease recurrence or death by 42 percent compared to treatment with the immunotherapy drug alone.

Georgina Long, medical director of the Australian Melanoma Institute and the study’s principal investigator, said in a statement that these results are “transformative” and “have the potential to make a foundational shift in how we treat patients with earlier-stage melanoma.”

The trial, known as INTerpath-001, included 1,137 people with high-risk melanoma who had undergone surgery to completely remove their tumors. The participants were divided into two groups. Those in the first group received only Pembrolizumab, a drug that helps the immune system detect and fight cancer cells, while those in the second group were treated with the same drug in combination with the mRNA vaccine, called Intismeran Autogene.

After monitoring the participants for an average of 24.6 months, the researchers determined that addition of the vaccine reduced the risk of cancer recurrence or death by 42 percent compared to Pembrolizumab alone.

The results also showed that the experimental treatment reduced the risk of the disease spreading to other parts of the body by 38 percent. At the end of the follow-up period, 82.2 percent of patients who received both treatments remained recurrence-free, compared with 70 percent of those who received Pembrolizumab alone.

“The results of the INTerpath-001 study represent a major breakthrough in the treatment of melanoma and, in particular, in the development of personalized medicine,” Eva Muñoz Couselo—who leads a melanoma research group at the Vall d’Hebron Institute of Oncology, in Barcelona and who was not involved in the study—told SMC Spain. “For the first time, a Phase III trial has demonstrated that an individualized therapeutic vaccine, designed on the basis of the molecular characteristics of each patient’s tumor, can significantly improve the outcomes of conventional immunotherapy.”

The laboratories explained that the vaccine was developed from a tumor sample taken from each patient, which was used to identify specific mutations that would create distinct proteins the immune system could learn to recognize in order to attack cancer cells. The scientists selected up to three of these molecular signatures per patient.

During the trial, some participants experienced mild side effects such as fever, fatigue, and pain at the injection site. These side effects were seen in earlier trials as well, and the Phase 3 findings demonstrate similar safety when the vaccine is used in the combination with Pembrolizumab.

“These results reinforce our conviction that mRNA has the potential to fundamentally change how we treat cancer,” Stéphane Bancel, CEO of Moderna, said in a statement.

The pharmaceutical companies emphasized that the vaccine is not designed to prevent melanoma but rather to reduce the risk of recurrence and death following prior medical treatment. They also noted that the therapy remains under investigation to validate its efficacy and obtain approval from health authorities before widespread use.

Most people diagnosed with melanoma have a high chance of survival, especially if detected early, but as one of the most common cancers, melanoma kills over 50,000 people globally each year. In theory, similar vaccines could be developed for other kinds of tumors in the future. Earlier this year, another trial demonstrated that personalized mRNA vaccines can greatly improve survival among patients with pancreatic cancer, one of the deadliest kinds of tumor.

“This is the major breakthrough, because conceptually it would make it possible to treat not only melanoma but also other tumors in which the immune system can be reactivated,” Marisol Soengas—a leading melanoma researcher at Spain’s National Cancer Research Center also not involved in the trial—told SMC Spain.

That doesn’t mean a cure for cancer is right around the corner. “The challenge is to ensure that this personalization can be scaled up to a large number of patients and that the cost is affordable for health care systems,” Soengas said. “It is logistically complex. It is literally a bespoke vaccine.”

This story originally appeared on WIRED en Español and has been translated from Spanish.

#495. This mysterious mushroom makes people see hundreds of tiny humans

People living thousands of miles apart have reported an extraordinarily strange experience after eating certain wild mushrooms. They begin seeing tiny human figures that appear to move through the real world, walking around objects, climbing over surfaces, and interacting with their surroundings.

These visions are known as Lilliputian hallucinations, named after the miniature inhabitants of "Gulliver's Travels," who stand just 6 inches tall. For decades, researchers suspected that cultural beliefs and folklore might explain why people in different regions described such similar experiences.

Now, a new study provides evidence that the phenomenon has a biological explanation.

Researchers at the University of Utah (U) used DNA sequencing to identify Lanmaoa asiatica as the mushroom responsible for these unusual hallucinations in Southwest China and the northern Philippines. Surprisingly, their investigation found no evidence of any known psychoactive mushroom compounds, including psilocybin.

The findings suggest that the mushroom may produce an entirely unfamiliar hallucinogenic substance. Identifying that chemical could help scientists better understand neurological disorders, unusual visual experiences, and the processes through which the brain creates our perception of reality.

The research was conducted by Colin Domnauer, a doctoral student at the U, and Bryn Dentinger, a mycologist affiliated with the university and the Natural History Museum of Utah. Their findings were published in the journal Mycologia.

In an interview with @theU, Domnauer explained how researchers traced the mushroom's identity, what makes its effects so unusual, and why the discovery could have implications far beyond the study of fungi.

A Century-Old Mystery of Tiny Human Hallucinations

The scientific mystery dates back to 1934, when Western researchers learned that people in Papua New Guinea sometimes experienced vivid visions of miniature humans after eating wild mushrooms. The bizarre effect became known as "mushroom madness."

During the 1950s and 1960s, anthropologists and mushroom specialists traveled to Papua New Guinea to investigate. Their work pointed toward several possible species belonging to the Boletaceae (bolete) family.

However, chemical testing failed to reveal any substances that could explain the hallucinations. Samples were even examined by Albert Hofmann, the scientist who first isolated psilocybin and discovered LSD. He, too, was unable to identify an active ingredient.

With no chemical explanation available, some researchers began questioning whether the mushrooms were responsible at all. They suggested that the visions might instead arise from cultural expectations or traditional beliefs. By the end of the 1960s, scientific investigation of the phenomenon had largely stopped.

The mystery resurfaced during the 1990s, when people in Yunnan, Southwest China, reported similar hallucinations after eating bolete mushrooms. Although the precise species remained uncertain, researchers suspected a recently described mushroom called Lanmaoa asiatica.

Scientists Identify the Mushroom Behind the Strange Visions

Another important clue appeared in 2024, when Domnauer learned of comparable experiences among an Indigenous community in the remote Northern Cordillera region of the Philippines.

Community members described a wild edible mushroom called "Sedesdem" that could sometimes cause people to see miniature human figures. Similar mushrooms were known locally as "Nonda" in Papua New Guinea and "Jian shou qing" in Yunnan.

These mushrooms have an important place in local food traditions, but consuming them without thoroughly cooking them can trigger the unusual hallucinations.

The Philippine accounts represented a third independent report of wild mushrooms causing remarkably similar visions of tiny people.

To investigate, Domnauer traveled to Yunnan and the Philippines, interviewed local residents, and collected specimens of L. asiatica along with other mushrooms belonging to the Lanmaoa genus.

The fieldwork included the first scientific survey of fungi in the northern Philippines. Researchers then analyzed DNA from the collected mushrooms to establish their identities and evolutionary relationships.

The results revealed that the mushrooms called Jian shou qing in China and Sedesdem in the Philippines were both L. asiatica.

This was an unexpected finding because scientists had previously believed the species occurred only in China.

Dentinger also visited Papua New Guinea in hopes of collecting specimens from the location where the mystery originally began. Unfortunately, unusually dry weather made it impossible to obtain samples for genetic comparison.

Although the Papua New Guinea mushroom remains unverified, the evidence from China and the Philippines provides a compelling connection. Three geographically separated cultures have described the same highly specific hallucinations, and DNA testing has linked two of those cases to a single mushroom species.

That consistency strongly suggests a shared chemical and neurological explanation rather than unrelated cultural influences.

Hallucinations of Tiny Humans That Seem Remarkably Real

What makes these hallucinations especially unusual is their striking similarity across different accounts.

People typically describe seeing dozens or even hundreds of miniature figures, generally measuring between 3 and 30 centimeters tall. The figures appear with extraordinary clarity, often seeming as solid and convincing as actual people.

According to Domnauer, approximately 90% of those experiencing the visions describe figures resembling elves, clowns, or other fairy-like characters, often wearing brightly colored clothing.

Even more surprising is the way these imaginary figures seem to behave.

Rather than floating through walls or ignoring their surroundings, they appear to follow familiar physical rules. They walk around obstacles, move across ordinary objects, and sometimes tumble from the edges of tables.

Domnauer recalled one particularly unusual account involving someone eating soup. The person reported seeing tiny humans leap from a spoon into the bowl, where they began swimming. When the person took another spoonful, the miniature figures reportedly remained visible inside their mouth.

Such detailed interactions distinguish these visions from many other forms of drug-induced hallucination.

A Mysterious Psychedelic Compound Unlike Any Known Drug

Scientists also investigated whether L. asiatica contains any of the chemicals already known to cause psychedelic experiences.

Using both chemical testing and genetic analysis, the researchers searched for established psychoactive compounds found in other mushroom species.

The absence of familiar hallucinogens was particularly intriguing because the mushroom's effects differ so dramatically from those associated with known psychedelic drugs.

Researchers now suspect that L. asiatica produces an unidentified psychoactive substance responsible for the tiny human hallucinations.

The compound has not yet been isolated or chemically identified, so its exact nature and mechanism remain unknown.

Discovering it could open an entirely new avenue of research into how certain chemicals alter visual perception.

What the Mushroom Could Reveal About the Human Brain

Although L. asiatica provides an unusually consistent trigger for Lilliputian hallucinations, the phenomenon is not limited to mushroom consumption.

Stories involving miniature humanlike beings appear throughout folklore around the world. Similar hallucinations have also been documented in people experiencing alcohol withdrawal, dementia, macular degeneration, and other neurological conditions.

These connections suggest that the visions may arise from a fundamental feature of the way the human brain processes visual information.

However, scientists still do not fully understand the neurological mechanisms responsible for these experiences or how best to treat them when they occur in medical settings.

This is where the mysterious mushroom could become especially valuable.

Unlike the neurological and medical conditions in which Lilliputian hallucinations occur relatively rarely, L. asiatica can produce the phenomenon in a more consistent and recognizable way.

If researchers can isolate the chemical responsible, they may gain a useful tool for investigating the brain circuits and processes that create these unusually detailed visual experiences.

Such research could eventually improve scientific understanding of hallucinations associated with neurological diseases.

The research team's next major objective is to isolate and identify the suspected psychedelic compound.

DNA Sequencing Reveals Four Previously Unknown Mushroom Species

The investigation also produced important discoveries about fungal biodiversity.

Rather than studying L. asiatica alone, Domnauer and Dentinger conducted the first comprehensive genomic analysis of the Lanmaoa genus.

They examined 53 mushroom specimens collected from natural habitats and preserved in fungarium collections around the world.

Genetic sequencing revealed 17 species within the group, including four species previously unknown to science.

Remarkably, two of those newly identified species had already been preserved in existing collections in the United States. Their distinct identities had gone unrecognized until researchers examined their DNA.

The analysis also indicated that L. asiatica is the only species in the genus currently known to produce psychoactive effects.

Scientists are now working to reconstruct the group's evolutionary history. They hope to determine how the unusual chemical trait developed and whether closely related mushrooms might produce comparable substances.

The findings also demonstrate the scientific value of museum collections. Specimens preserved for years can contain biological discoveries that become apparent only when newer analytical techniques are applied.

For Domnauer, the hidden diversity found in these collections is a reminder that many species and potentially important biological characteristics remain undiscovered, even among organisms that scientists have already collected.

Exploring Fungi Could Lead to New Medicines and Discoveries

The broader implications extend well beyond unusual hallucinations.

Fungi remain one of the least thoroughly explored groups of organisms, and researchers believe their chemical diversity could provide valuable clues about biology, medicine, and the nervous system.

One of the most famous examples is penicillin, the antibiotic originally obtained from a fungus that transformed the treatment of bacterial infections and became one of the most consequential medical discoveries of the last century.

Scientists hope continued exploration of fungal biodiversity could reveal other medically useful compounds and previously unknown biological processes.

The mysterious effects of L. asiatica illustrate just how much remains to be learned about the fungal kingdom. A mushroom long consumed by communities in Asia may contain a substance capable of revealing new information about the human brain and how it creates conscious experience.

The research received funding from the National Science Foundation (award #DEB-2114785).

#493. Christina Applegate Shares Health Update, Vows to Keep Fighting for MS Community: “I Will Speak Out on Your Behalf Forever”

The 54-year-old actress revealed she was misdiagnosed with the disease last week.

Despite being misdiagnosed with multiple sclerosis, commonly referred to as MS, Christina Applegate says she will continue to be a “warrior” for the community.

On Tuesday’s episode of Applegate and co-host Jamie-Lynn Sigler’s MeSsY podcast, Applegate reflected on her recent revelation that she doesn’t have MS, saying she’ll keep advocating for those living with the disease.

“Even though I don’t have this disease, I’ve fallen in love with everyone that we know, our listeners, our friends that have MS. I’m still your warrior, and I’m still going to speak out on your behalf forever and ever. Despite not having MS, I still feel like part of this community. No matter what, I love you,” the Married… with Children star said. Related Stories News Christina Applegate Reveals She Was Misdiagnosed and Does Not Have MS: "I Haven't Gotten Better ... I'm Back at Square One" General News Christina Applegate Breaks Silence Amid Hospitalization Reports to Give Health Update

In 2021, Applegate revealed she had been diagnosed with MS. She retired from acting in 2022 following the finale of the Netflix series Dead to Me. Since then, the 54-year-old actress has advocated for the MS community and launched the MeSsY podcast with Sopranos star Sigler, who was diagnosed with MS in 2001.

Last week, however, Applegate announced that she recently learned she was misdiagnosed. “You don’t get misdiagnosed, and then all of a sudden, I’m better,” she said on MeSsY. “I haven’t gotten better, Jamie. If anything, I’ve gotten worse in some ways. So I’m back at square one where I don’t know what’s going on with me, and that’s going to be an online investigation that my doctors are going to get into to find out what has been happening since 2021.”

Elsewhere in this week’s MeSsY episode, Applegate also shared an update on her health, describing the symptoms of what she believes may be another undiagnosed illness.

“I actually timed it today; it took me 10 minutes to be able to move off my pillow and get out of the bed and stand up, at which point I almost couldn’t walk to the bathroom, and now a walker is sitting next to my bed because of what’s going on. I don’t know what’s going on,” Applegate said. “This is what I’m still living with, and I never had that before. I was able to sit up and stuff.”

She added that she recently lost “too much” weight and said, “I don’t know what’s going on.”

“The more you lose in your face, the older you look. Like, the jowls are more pronounced now than they were before because it’s all hollow and I just look older,” she explained.
