#468. US sanctions target Iran's rail sector as Tehran shifts cargo from sea routes to overland corridors

As US sanctions expand to Iran's rail sector, Tehran is relying more heavily on overland links with China and Central Asia. But capacity constraints, delays and costs limit rail's ability to replace maritime trade.

The United States has opened another front in its economic pressure campaign against Iran, this time targeting the railways Tehran increasingly relies on as maritime trade becomes more difficult.

On October 1, the US Treasury sanctioned three Iranian railway companies alongside seven automakers and five foreign suppliers of automotive parts as part of its Operation Economic Outcast.

Washington says Iran's rail and automotive industries provide revenue and logistical capacity to the state and are connected to networks involving the Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps (IRGC).

The timing of the move is significant. Since the US maritime blockade sharply restricted Iran's southern trade routes, Tehran has tried to move more goods through its land borders and railway connections with China, Central Asia, Russia and Turkey.

Rail transport has become more important just as Washington decided to target it.

Shahriar Naghizadeh, director general for foreign trade at Iran's railway authority, was quoted by the semi-official Mehr news agency as saying that the country has around 15,000 kilometers (9,300 miles) of main and secondary railway lines, more than 35,000 wagons and nearly 500 diesel locomotives.

The network carried around 44 million tons of freight in the Iranian year ending in March 2026, up from about 40 million tons the previous year, according to Naghizadeh. And authorities are targeting 54 million tons this year.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Iran is linked to neighboring markets through 13 rail crossings, providing overland access to Turkey, Azerbaijan, Afghanistan, Pakistan and onward routes to Central Asia and China via Turkmenistan.

The new US sanctions raise questions about how foreign railway operators, freight forwarders, banks and insurers will continue dealing with Iranian rail companies.

Bloomberg reported in May that freight trains traveling from the Chinese city of Xi'an to Tehran had increased from around one per week before the US blockade to roughly one every three or four days.

In September, a train left China carrying automotive parts, consumer goods and other cargo. It ran through Kazakhstan, Uzbekistan and Turkmenistan before entering Iran at Sarakhs with an expected transit time of 14 to 16 days.

For Iran, such services offer a route to Chinese suppliers that avoids the area around the Strait of Hormuz. But advertised transit times do not necessarily reflect what an importer experiences.

A Chinese freight company told DW that actual delivery times can stretch far beyond advertised schedules because of border inspections, customs procedures and train availability.

It estimated that rail transit along the main route from western China to Tehran would take 50-60 days under current conditions. Road transport along a similar route is around 22 to 25 days.

The gap between a scheduled 14-day transit time and commercial estimates of up to 60 days highlights one of the central challenges facing Iran.

Customs inspections, gauge changes, payment problems and sanctions can all be another potential point of delay.

Increasing the number of trains also requires more locomotives, wagons and cooperation from several transit countries.

Alireza Salavati, managing director of London-based Middle East Analytica, said rail is increasingly useful for Iran but should not be mistaken as a replacement for maritime trade.

"A workable overland route now links China and Iran, but I would not call it a replacement for sea freight," he told DW.

Under normal conditions, ships can move far larger quantities at lower unit costs. Rail becomes more attractive for goods where speed and continuity matter more than the cheapest transport.

That includes automotive components, machinery, electronics and industrial inputs, whose absence can stop an entire production line.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Salavati argues that the effect on companies cannot be measured only in freight charges.

For a manufacturer importing $1 million (€890,000) worth of inputs every 30 days, a 30-day journey means roughly a million dollars of inventory may be tied up in transit.

Cutting the journey to 15 days could reduce that amount significantly, depending on payment arrangements. The reverse can be much more damaging.

If input costs rise while delivery times stretch from 30 to 45 days, companies need more working capital simply to maintain the same production flow, the expert explained, pointing out that a factory can have customers and orders but still have to reduce production because too much cash is trapped in goods that have not arrived.

"Reliability matters as much as speed," Salavati said. "A 15-day train is little use if the container waits a week to depart, or if the bank or insurer will not touch it."

The new US sanctions add another source of uncertainty to that calculation.

Optimizing its railway use would give Iran more options than having to rely on southern ports alone, but the overall scale remains limited.

Washington's sanction strategy is designed to isolate Iran by closing maritime, financial, aviation and now railway channels.

Still, Salavati argues that the same pressure may accelerate Iran's integration with alternative networks.

"Washington's efforts to isolate Iran may also be pushing it deeper than at any point in recent years into China's economic orbit and closer to Russia's wartime strategic system," he said.

That does not mean China or Russia can make up for the economic cost of US pressure, since Iranian importers still need insurers, banks and customers able to absorb higher prices.

Container trains can only carry a fraction of what a large cargo vessel can transport, so for now, rail provides Iran with a limited alternative trade channel.

Its wider success depends on better financing and becoming more reliable — but even then it is not a wholesale substitute for sea freight.

#467. Archaeologists may have just found the world's oldest shipwreck

A shipwreck off the coast of Turkey is the oldest shipwreck in the world, our team of archaeologists believes.

There’s little left of the ship itself but based on clues from its cargo – including pottery vessels – a research team I was part of has tentatively dated the wreck to around 2700–2400 BCE.

That’s about 4,500 years ago. Prior to this discovery, the world’s oldest shipwreck was a wreck off the coast of Dokos, in Greece, thought to have been from about 4,200 years ago.

Much work remains to be done to finesse the conclusions, and a paper outlining the results is yet to be published in a peer-reviewed journal. Yet the discovery could transform our understanding of ancient Mediterranean connections during the Early Bronze Age.

Here, and perhaps even earlier, lie the foundations of extensive trade routes as we know them today.

Associate Professor Hakan Öniz of Akdeniz University, Antalya, has worked along this coastline for decades excavating ancient shipwrecks.

Acting on clues from local seafarers, Öniz found a shipwreck in shallow waters off Kekova, west of Antalya in Turkey, in 2024. Earlier this year, he contacted me and asked if I would help date some of the pottery vessels found on this wreck.

As an archaeologist specializing in desert sites, this was a singular experience. New technology, water-logged methods of excavation and laborious conservation techniques were the new normal. Yet there is always room for learning new skills in life.

Of the ship itself there is now nothing to see, owing to underwater exposure. Yet the research team has now estimated the ship and its cargo dates to around 2700–2400 BCE. However, the precise dating is still under consideration. Earlier or later dates on either side of this initial determination are also possible.

This discovery promises to re-write our understanding of early trade networks from around 4,500 years ago.

The ship is a “missing piece” in the story of networks, trade and commodity exchange in the wider eastern Mediterranean. This era includes the pyramid age of Giza and its mighty pharaohs.

What remains under the sea is a time capsule of ancient trade.

Although its direction was not known, the boat was evidently traveling along the coast of southern Turkey.

Now resting on the seabed between two islands, it is apparent the ship likely sought refuge in the lee waters during a storm. One could imagine the terror of the sailors – possibly coastal people from the Levant region – as their ship crashed against the rocks.

While timbers from the ship disappeared long ago, the remains of its precious cargo were embedded in the rocky slope along the Kekova coast.

Divers worked over the past four months to extract its pottery from barnacles clinging to the rocky underwater slope.

Pottery is like plastic: it never goes away and just breaks down into smaller and smaller pieces.

Yet humble pottery vessels hold many clues about the past.

In this case, the cargo included large ceramic storage vessels or amphorae which once contained products from regions of the eastern Mediterranean.

The pots were large ovoid, flat-based jars with two handles, many featuring a raised horizontal clay “belt” around the widest part of the jar.

Through these vessels was possible to ascertain many pieces of information. This includes the likely origin of the jars and the contents exchanged across the region.

Importantly, the shape and manufacturing technique of the pots themselves help us date the cargo. By extension, this includes also the date of other pottery, and the ship itself.

Compared to later periods, trade and exchange in the eastern Mediterranean during the third millennium (3000–2000 BCE) is poorly understood.

Maritime voyages during the that period are known from Egyptian texts, pottery vessels and raw materials from ancient tombs.

Around the same time as this ship struck the rocks, the ancient Egyptians were trading in the eastern Mediterranean through the port of Byblos in Lebanon.

Commodities such as cedar, tin/lead, oils, tree resins, lapis lazuli and silver, all materials that Egypt lacked, were imported by the Egyptian state.

Recent scientific research also reveals that silver was acquired by the Egyptian state around 2600 BCE. Analyses showed this silver was acquired from the Lavrion mines east of modern Athens.

While it is unlikely Egyptian ships made this journey, boats like the Kekova ship may have used the route as part of an extended international maritime network.

And what was in the jars? Remarkably there were still residues visible to the naked eye. Again, scientific analyses are pending, but could be some of the products mentioned in the Egyptian texts, possibly olive oil, wine or tree resin.

The ship may have acquired its pottery from several ports on their journey. Our best assessment at this stage of research is that some of the “belted” jars (or clays used to make them) may have originated in the Central Jordan Valley.

The coast of southern Turkey is littered with ancient shipwrecks. While maritime archaeology is a relatively recent phenomenon, some of the world’s most famous wrecks have been discovered there. Who knows what other secrets these waters hold.

The research on which this article is based was led by Associate Professor Hakan Öniz, head of the Division of Mediterranean Underwater Cultural Heritage Research, and the Department of Restoration and Conservation of Cultural Heritage in the Faculty of Art at the Akdeniz University, Antalya, Turkey.

Materials provided by The Conversation. Original written by Karin Sowada, Senior Lecturer in Archaeology and Director of the Australian Centre for Egyptology, Macquarie University. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#465. Hyve Smart Package Locker Review: Protecting Your Holiday Deliveries

The onslaught of tech gear being delivered for WIRED Reviews, combined with the samples I receive constantly for my side hustle at Drinkhacker, means that delivery trucks are a constant occurrence on my suburban street. UPS and FedEx both make drops at my house nearly every day, and that’s not to mention occasional deliveries from the likes of DHL and, of course, Amazon and its ilk. But it’s usually not a problem. I work from home, so picking up packages left on the doorstep is just part of the scenery.

What is a problem is that I can’t be home 24 hours a day, and even though I have a very kind neighbor across the way who keeps an eye out for boxes on my front steps when I’m traveling, he isn’t always at home either. Package hold requests aren’t consistently honored by either of the major carriers—and earlier this year, FedEx seemingly discontinued its vacation hold option without bothering to tell anyone, the modern epidemic of porch piracy be damned. (A rep has since confirmed this with me, though no formal announcement has ever been made.)

Having packages held at shipping centers or access points usually costs money, typically about $5 per package and often more depending on the length of the hold, and that involves a trip after I get home to collect what’s arrived. If I’m gone for a week, that can mean fetching a dozen boxes or more.

All of that is a time-consuming pain, for which a startup called Hyve has come along to offer an assuagement: A smart lockbox that lets delivery drivers drop off packages in a secure location, notifying the homeowner whenever it’s been accessed. While the growing pains were significant, after weeks of testing, I found it a useful device that—just maybe—may earn a permanent home on my front porch.

Parcel lockboxes aren’t exactly a new concept. They operate basically like a standard post office mailbox, with a cantilevered hatch up top that allows deliveries to drop into a secure storage area below, where they can only be accessed through a keyed door. Delivery folks can put packages in through the hatch, but only you can get them out.

The Hyve Smart Package Locker is a play on this idea, forgoing the two-chamber design in favor of an electronic hatch accessed via 4-digit PINs. The box itself measures 32 inches high, 20 inches wide, and 20 inches deep, with interior dimensions about 4 inches shorter in each direction. That makes for a total of 7.4 cubic feet, which Hyve says will fit 80 percent of Amazon package sizes (though obviously this depends on the specifics of your ordering habits). For me, that math worked out: Deliveries of three-pack bottles of wine fit fine, but anything larger was typically left out by the courier.

It’s built from a combination of plastic and steel, and while the side panels seem a little flimsy, there’s enough reinforcement behind them that I never worried that the unit would be easily damaged by a would-be package thief without power tools. Units are available in gray or white; I requested the latter to match my home’s color, but alas received a gray one.

Assembly is required—and be warned, it’s no cakewalk. The locker comes packed flat, notably with no printed instructions on how to put all the pieces together. A QR code in the box takes you to a web page featuring a walkthrough video and a PDF guide, which are my two least favorite ways to be guided through an installation process on a cell phone. The PDF is very difficult to read on a small screen, and this contributed to making what was already a cumbersome construction process even more difficult. I consider myself quite handy, but I ultimately spent nearly an hour putting the locker together due to a couple of easy-to-overlook goofs. Real, printed instructions—especially considering they’re all of five pages long—would go a long way toward streamlining the process. Or, better yet, why not just ship the thing preassembled?

A security bracket and cable tether are included so you can attach the Hyve to an immovable object on your property to prevent pirates from simply running off with the entire thing. (Unloaded, the device weighs 40 pounds. While awkward, it would be fairly easy to abscond with if it isn’t physically locked down.) Though oddly, the installation of these components is covered only in the video, not in the written instructions.

The unit is powered by four AA batteries and features a solar panel on top for trickle charging a backup battery. There’s also a USB-C port that can be used to supply emergency power if all else fails. Hyve says a fresh set of cells should last about five or six months, though strangely, my first quartet of AAs died inside of three weeks and without warning. There’s fortunately a USB-C port that can be used to supply emergency power if all else fails (which I needed when my cells died).

The Hyve Locker requires its own Wi-Fi bridge, which you must plug into an inside outlet near where you’ll be placing the locker. I found this to be a bit of a tall order, as I don’t have any power outlets in my entry hall, and an outlet only 15 feet away from the locker turned out to be out of range. (Exterior walls will do that to a wireless signal.) I ended up finding another outlet, forgotten in the guest room, that was able to reach the Hyve, but if that hadn’t worked, I would have been forced to relocate the locker elsewhere. Fortunately, the signal has held strong over several weeks of testing.

Hyve’s app is deceptively simple. At its most basic, the homeowner sets a master PIN, which everyone uses for access to the locker to both make deliveries and to retrieve them. It’s a simple idea, but it obviously presents a bit of a security risk, since anyone with the four-digit code can pop the lid open and rifle through what’s inside. Whether this is a legitimate concern or not is perhaps a matter of personal risk tolerance. Due to the physical design of the device, there’s nothing preventing one courier from removing a package that another has left inside the Hyve, but then again, there’s nothing preventing them from taking an unattended package off your doorstep, either. Presumably for Hyve, and I would agree, random passers-by are the bigger concern for most homeowners, so the monolithic PIN mode is probably good enough for many users.

If you want more fine-grained security than the mono-PIN, you’ll need a subscription plan. Hyve’s Essentials subscription plan runs $5 per month or $50 per year, the main feature being that you can set separate PINs for each delivery service or carrier (including major shippers as well as services including DoorDash, Instacart, Best Buy, and more). With this setup, the app notifies you each time the lid is opened, and it tells you who opened it (or at least who employs them), logging it in the system. One month of Essentials is included with purchase.

What do you do with all these PINs? Alas, you’ll have to go it alone, though Hyve provides detailed advice on how to enter the PIN and information about the lockbox into each carrier’s delivery instructions page on their website. You’ll need an account with each carrier, tied to your address, and I found that some of these instructions are more useful than others. Both FedEx and UPS, for example, provide fields for entering gate codes, but neither lets you specify specifically that packages should be left in a lockbox. (Both used to offer free-form delivery instruction fields, but these have recently changed into more simplified drop-down systems, and “lockbox” is not a choice for a delivery location.)

If you don’t have the Hyve Returns subscription but do have Essentials, you can still use the Hyve for on-demand returns by scheduling a pickup through Uber Courier, with no additional charges due to Hyve. The trick with this scheme is that while you avoid the $15 subscription fee, you have to pay Uber’s service charge for the pickup. Those fees vary based on the distance to your nearest UPS, FedEx, or Amazon drop-off location. For me, the Uber Courier charge for dropping off an Amazon return typically ranged from $8–18, making the $15 twice-monthly return plan far more enticing than the ad hoc option. (But alas, unavailable to me.)

I did test the alternate method by using Uber Courier to create a return, and found that Hyve didn’t add much to the equation. The only difference with Hyve is that instead of grabbing the item from the doorstep, the driver fetched it from the bin with a PIN. More secure? Yes, but given that I only had to wait 20 minutes for the driver to show up, the risk of an exposed package was minimal compared to receiving a delivery when I wasn’t home for days.

While the lockbox is rather obtrusive on my front porch, the hardware works well. The weatherproofing is fine, the door opens and closes with a satisfying, locking click, and the box sounds an alarm if it’s left open for too long. Push notifications from the Hyve mobile app come quickly and accurately, letting you know whether it was FedEx or Amazon—or you—that opened the hatch.

There is, unfortunately, a catch, and at first I found it to be an awfully big one. The problem was that no delivery driver seemed to want to use the Hyve locker, no matter how much I tried to get them to.

After getting everything set up on day one, I waited with anticipation when FedEx arrived with a box, and my phone lit up with a text from the company that the delivery had been completed. Alas, it was not in the Hyve locker but sitting next to it, nestled against the exterior. I was crestfallen, yet undeterred, and kept trying to get someone to open that hatch.

The next day, I encountered the FedEx driver in person, only because he rang the bell because he had a delivery with a signature required. I asked if he had seen the delivery instructions on my FedEx account and, with a puzzled look, said that he “thought he heard something” when pulling up to my house. It turns out that FedEx’s driver devices provide verbal instructions on where to leave packages (in addition to rather garbled written ones, which he showed me), but that he had the volume on his device turned down. Clad in my bathrobe, I demonstrated the Hyve to him personally and got his buy-in.

The following day, I was thrilled to get a push notification from the Hyve app that FedEx had successfully left a box for me inside. The driver even rang the bell again, only to show me his success with a smile. I tell you, opening that hatch and seeing my package inside was like Christmas morning for both of us.

Alas, on the third day, a fresh FedEx delivery appeared on the porch again. My driver had moved to another route.

I was about to give up on the whole shebang when, all of a sudden, something clicked. FedEx, UPS, and Amazon all started using the Hyve locker, and regularly. I soon became accustomed to getting notifications multiple times a day that the box had received a delivery, with my estimation of its usage rate approaching 75 percent. No longer was my doorstep darkened with cardboard, as trips to empty out the Hyve became an undertaking necessary to complete several times a day.

In fact, on some days, the usage level revealed an entirely new problem: A single UPS delivery of a few boxes could fill the locker, leaving no room for anything else. My hopes of leaving for a week and not having to worry about packages were dashed; there’s just not enough space for more than a day or two at most in the bin, given the pace of deliveries at my house.

Early on in my testing, I spoke to Hyve about the usage issues I was experiencing at the time, and its CEO freely admitted that this is, in no uncertain terms, a problem for many homeowners. To combat it, the company provides an adhesive sign that you can use to instruct carriers to use the box, and while this was likely quite helpful at improving usage rates, it’s not a panacea (nor is it particularly attractive).

And over a Zoom call, the CEO showed me another innovation that she said was having even better results: Attaching a placard that reads “Snack Stop” to the top of the lockbox, and stocking a collection of treats like chips and meat sticks inside for hungry drivers, encouraging them to open it up to grab a bite when making their rounds. What delivery driver could possibly say no to a free Slim Jim?

#463. Solar Cells Inspected on NASA’s NEO Surveyor

The large sunshade of NASA’s Near-Earth Object (NEO) Surveyor mission is inspected by a technician at BAE Systems Space & Mission Systems in Boulder, Colorado, in late July.

Built by Applied Aerospace in Stockton, California, the sunshade structure was outfitted with its solar cells by SolAero Technologies in Albuquerque, New Mexico, in January. Environmental testing of the sunshade and solar array was completed at a commercial thermal vacuum testing facility in El Segundo, California, in March and it was delivered to BAE Systems Space & Mission Systems ahead of schedule in May.

When in space, NEO Surveyor will seek out some of the hardest-to-find asteroids and comets, some of which may be located in the direction of the Sun — a region of sky where ground-based optical telescopes have a harder time searching.

More information about NEO Surveyor is available at:

https://science.nasa.gov/mission/neo-surveyor/

#460. Cowboy Space unveils 'Big Iron' rocket to launch 20,000 AI data centers

Cowboy Space aims to launch the 282-foot-tall (86 meters) Big Iron for the first time in late 2028.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

PALO ALTO, Calif. — Cowboy Space just showed us how it plans to become a big player in the final frontier.

The California-based company, which is run by Robinhood co-founder Baiju Bhatt, intends to operate a constellation of 20,000 AI data centers in low Earth orbit (LEO). It will build those satellites in house and launch them on a homegrown rocket — a vehicle Cowboy Space unveiled here on Monday night (Oct. 26), at an event it called "Big Sky Rising."

"It's a fundamentally novel approach to rocketry in the modern era, in our opinion," Bhatt said during the event. "The idea is that you have a two-stage rocket where the upper stage of the rocket itself transforms into the satellite."

That rocket is called Big Iron, Bhatt told us on Monday night. And so is the data-center satellite, as both are considered to be part of the same system.

The stainless-steel rocket will be 282 feet (86 meters) tall and 15.4 feet (4.7 m) wide. It will be powered by Cowboy Space's Clydesdale engines — nine in the first stage and one in the upper stage, the same configuration employed by SpaceX's workhorse Falcon 9 rocket. And, like the Raptor engines that power another SpaceX launcher, the giant Starship, Clydesdale will burn liquid oxygen and liquid methane, a combo known as methalox.

Big Iron's first stage will be reusable; an animation Bhatt showed during "Big Sky Rising" showed the booster coming down for a landing on a ship at sea, as Falcon 9 first stages often do these days.

The upper stage, meanwhile, will stay in orbit as a Big Iron satellite. The spacecraft will tip the scales here on Earth at about 33,000 pounds (15,000 kilograms), and, after unfurling its solar panels, it will be about 433 feet (132 m) long.

The payload fairing that protected the satellite during launch will morph into a radiator, which will dump into space the prodigious heat generated by Big Iron's GPUs (general processing units). And there will be a lot of GPUs: Each satellite will carry five racks of them, Bhatt said, which will provide about 1 megawatt of compute payload.

Big Iron will therefore be much larger than an "imperial-class satellite" — Bhatt's term for SpaceX's planned Starmind AI data center spacecraft, each of which will have a solar wingspan of 246 feet (75 m) and boast about 250 kW of compute payload.

Cowboy Space plans to operate about 20,000 Big Iron satellites in LEO, a constellation it calls Stampede. Each rocket will carry just one of these satellites to orbit, so it will take 20,000 launches to build Stampede — and, likely, several thousand more every year to maintain it at that number.

Cowboy Space therefore intends to launch very often — multiple times per day, from launch complexes it calls Trailheads. The company has not yet announced where these complexes will be, but each one will likely feature four active launch mounts, Bhatt said on Monday night.

And not all of these launches will serve Stampede. Toward the end of "Big Sky Rising," Bhatt announced that Big Iron will also launch payloads for customers, a service that Cowboy Space calls Big Iron Express.

The first flights of this new rocket are a few years away. Cowboy Space — which was founded in 2024 as the power-beaming company Aetherflux — is still building and testing its Clydesdale engine and says it aims to launch Big Iron for the first time in December 2028.

"Now, a lot of things have to go right for that to happen; it's not a guarantee," Bhatt said. "I think it's ambitious. But if it wasn't ambitious, I wouldn't be an entrepreneur."

Cowboy Space is operating one piece of space hardware at the moment, by the way — a satellite called Reason-1, which launched on SpaceX's Transporter-18 rideshare mission on Oct. 1.

"Its job is to prove out power beaming: using a high-power laser to transfer energy from low Earth orbit to a receiver on the ground," Cowboy Space said via X on Sept. 28. "If you want to build data centers in space, you have to master the optical systems between orbit and Earth. This is where that journey begins."

Michael Wall is the Spaceflight and Tech Editor for Space.com and joined the team in 2010. He primarily covers human and robotic spaceflight, military space, and exoplanets, but has been known to dabble in the space art beat. His book about the search for alien life, "Out There," was published on Nov. 13, 2018. Before becoming a science writer, Michael worked as a herpetologist and wildlife biologist. He has a Ph.D. in evolutionary biology from the University of Sydney, Australia, a bachelor's degree from the University of Arizona, and a graduate certificate in science writing from the University of California, Santa Cruz. #459. Russia frees jailed American Stephen Hubbard during Witkoff talks

During talks between Vladimir Putin and Steve Witkoff, Putin announced that jailed American Stephen Hubbard had been released. The Kremlin also said Russia was "in principle" ready to resume trilateral talks next month.

Russian President Vladimir Putin announced the release of US captive Stephen Hubbard during talks with US special envoy Steve Witkoff in St. Petersburg on Wednesday.

Hubbard had been taken captive by Russian forces on charges of being a mercenary, which he denies. He was sentenced to seven years in prison at a closed trial in 2024.

Putin said Hubbard was at the airport and Witkoff said he was now "going home to his family."

US President Donald Trump later said he had made the request directly to Putin during a phone call last week.

"The United States, out of respect for President Trump, was not required to give anything in return — an unprecedented situation for Russia," Trump claimed in a post on his Truth Social platform. "There was no 'swap' necessary for this release."

However, in a follow-up post, Trump also claimed that "numerous people" had called him to apologize after they criticized him for suspending a new round of sanctions, which he said was a necessary concession.

"If sanctions would have been placed, certainly those negotiations would have come to a complete and total halt," Trump's post said.

The release of Hubbard was seen as a goodwill gesture to the Trump administration as the two sides discuss a deal for peace between Russia and Ukraine.

"We've brought some papers. It's really some ideas, some sort of peace deal between Russia and Ukraine, and you'll read that at your leisure," Witkoff told Putin.

After the talks, the Kremlin said Witkoff's proposals "lacked substance" and would not be of real use for a potential peace settlement.

However, Putin agreed "in principle" to resume peace talks with Russian, Ukrainian and US delegates after November 10.

Such talks should focus on "building a security system for Russia and Ukraine after the conflict ends," Putin's foreign affairs adviser Yuri Ushakov said.

Putin also proposed that Witkoff discuss the restoration of bilateral ties between Washington and Moscow.

Meanwhile, Putin appeared open to a potential trilateral meeting with Trump and Chinese President Xi Jinping in Beijing.

"A possible first-ever meeting of the leaders of China, Russia and the United States... is being prepared," Ushakov said.

The meeting — the ninth such talks between Witkoff and Putin — came a day after Witkoff met with Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelenskyy in Norway.

Zelenskyy on Wednesday called for freezing the front line as a first step toward ceasefire talks.

"Right now we have to freeze, and I think this is (the) real way to end this war. We call it ceasefire... freeze and stop," Zelenskyy said in an interview with "Fox & Friends."

The European Union, which was represented by delegates from several member states at the Oslo talks on Tuesday, said it expected to be debriefed by Washington after Wednesday's talks.

German Chancellor Friedrich Merz also said on Wednesday that Berlin was supporting Ukraine to end the war as quickly as possible, adding that any capitulation by Ukraine would be "a capitulation by all of us."

The Ukrainian leader remained in Oslo on Wednesday where met with lawmakers in the Norwegian parliament.

"I think we may have entered a phase now where the Russians are also looking for opportunities for diplomacy," he told Norway's public broadcaster NRK on Wednesday morning.

#458. Netflix has dipped its toe into theatrical. Is it ready to jump in?

Netflix is making itself clear: It's a streaming-first business.

Yet, the company is set to have 30 theatrical releases in 2027, on par with what it brought to cinemas in 2026. And while not all of these features are expected to have wide releases or long runs in theaters, Netflix is testing the waters with projects like Greta Gerwig's "Narnia: The Magician's Nephew," which will have a wide release and a 49-day exclusive theatrical window when it hits cinemas in February.

The company seems poised to carve a new path forward as it grapples with viewership stagnation and a stock slump of roughly 30% since January.

How its theatrical ambitions fit into a streaming-first strategy remains a question mark.

"Theatrical releases are a net positive for reach and streaming demand," Alicia Reese, analyst at Wedbush, wrote in a note Wednesday. "Netflix's more amenable attitude toward exclusive theatrical windows will not only facilitate better relationships with talent, but also add a new revenue stream and build interest in its streaming service. This follows the pattern we have seen with the major studios' streaming platforms over the past several years: theatrical releases don't cannibalize the streaming window, as Netflix fears."

Ahead of its Tuesday earnings report, Netflix restructured its C-suite, bringing in former Disney executive Peter Rice to take over as the company's chief content officer, ousting 10-year veteran Bela Bajaria.

Netflix also dismissed Dan Lin, the company's head of film, according to a person familiar with the matter who spoke on the condition of anonymity to discuss internal changes. That came just days after it laid off roughly 1,000 employees including Lin's right hand Doug Belgrad as well as Sheroum Kim, the director of independent film, according to various media reports. It's unclear who Netflix will tap to take over Lin's position.

The company aims to restore quality to the brand and utilize Rice's expertise in talent relations to lure big names in an increasingly competitive market.

Still, Netflix executives seemed to downplay the change in leadership during the company's earnings call.

"Our business and our business flywheel are working," Sarandos told investors during the company's earnings call. "There is no need for a fundamental shift in the strategy. We do need better execution in the U.S. ... It's just not growing as fast as it could or as fast as we'd like."

Sarandos said the streamer had "some really solid doubles and triples this year," but suggested the company needs more "home runs."

In an emblematic moment during Tuesday's call, Sarandos highlighted a deal with Disney, struck in October, to bring the Ice Age films and others to the streaming service, and gave a nod to that company's CEO, Josh D'Amaro.

"We already are seeing some of those benefits from the Ice Age movies. All five of them hit our top 10. That's really exciting," the Netflix co-CEO said. "And by the way, yes, Netflix just plugged a Disney movie on our earnings call. You're welcome, Josh."

But more recently, it's lacked some of the water-cooler-type hits like "Stranger Things," "Bridgerton" and "Squid Game" that previously boosted subscriptions and viewership numbers.

"We know there's some investor concern about our view hours growth," Netflix said in its shareholder letter Tuesday. "Engagement — which encompasses quantity, quality and variety of viewing — remains healthy."

And yet with Netflix stating plainly that it needs new avenues for growth — and against the backdrop of a foiled deal to buy the Warner Bros. film studio — investors and onlookers are once again looking at the theatrical business.

"I think there's a place for the theatrical for Netflix," Matthew Condon, equity research analyst at Citizens, told CNBC. "Especially, as the competition for time and attention just continues to increase."

"If they really want to build stickier fan bases around some of their [intellectual property], I think theatrical could be a good avenue for them to do that," Condon said. "That's one area that Netflix has historically not been all that strong in, is building like really, really strong IP. And I think building fan bases around theatrical releases and things of that nature can help with that and then also can help them get top tier talent."

Netflix is no stranger to luring in big Hollywood directors. It's worked with the likes of Rian Johnson, Martin Scorsese, Alfonso Cuarón, Bong Joon Ho, Spike Lee and Guillermo del Toro. However, none of these directors' Netflix films received a wide theatrical release or a long run in theaters.

Sarandos admitted earlier this month that the company has lost talent because it would not guarantee wide theatrical releases.

Most notably, the creators and lead producer behind its hit "Stranger Things" have all jumped ship in the last year. Matt and Ross Duffer signed a four-year exclusive deal with Skydance — the new home of the same Warner Bros. film studio that Netflix nearly bought — while Shawn Levy joined the ranks of Disney to direct "Star Wars: Starfighter."

Sarandos said Tuesday the company's strategy is about "specialty theatrical releasing," in which each film gets its own bespoke release. In some cases, Netflix plays the film in theaters for the minimum requirement to be eligible for awards. Others appear for weekend stints as one-off events, like in the case of its "KPop Demon Hunters" smash that ran in theaters for just two days.

Gerwig's "Narnia" adaptation is among those chosen few to get longer theatrical runs and a wider reach, along with an animated film called "Charlie vs. the Chocolate Factory."

"Both films have big global IP and big global fandom, so we're very excited about how they'll play out next year," Sarandos said.

In another sign of its evolution into theaters, Netflix has said it will report box office results for films that have longer theatrical windows. Those readouts are standard for major studios, regardless of release or windowing, but the move marks a new level of disclosure for the streamer.

Incoming content head Rice could be the missing piece for Netflix in a new theatrical model.

The executive has a wealth of experience in television and film. Since 2023 he has produced films like "Saturday Night" and "28 Years Later." He's currently developing a movie adaptation of the video game "Elden Ring."

But right now, Netflix doesn't have the infrastructure for a full theatrical commitment.

"Going into theatrical isn't quite as easy as I think sometimes folks make it sound," Steven Cahall, stock analyst at Wells Fargo, told CNBC. "You have to negotiate with movie theaters, and find spots for your films."

Cahall noted that sending the occasional film to theaters, especially with a limited release, "maybe isn't so hard, but there is a muscle of theatrical distribution ... You need a team of people, and you need a pipeline of content that's intended for theatrical release, and you need a marketing department around it."

That's where buying Warner Bros. Discovery's studio assets would have come in. In the absence of that acquisition, it's not clear how, or how quickly, Netflix can bulk up its own operations.

A shift to a multi-pronged strategy that feeds both streaming and cinemas would require Netflix to more closely mimic the companies it disrupted in the first place.

"So that becomes more of an existential question for Netflix: Is it time to become a media conglomerate rather than being a pure play streaming company?" Wells Fargo's Cahall said. "'Do we want to become a media conglomerate?'"

#457. Tiny magnetic vortices could help computers work more like your brain

Tiny magnetic structures called skyrmions could help pave the way for a new generation of computers that process information more like the human brain while using far less electricity. Although most people have never heard of skyrmions, these microscopic magnetic vortices may eventually play an important role in how computers store and process data.

A study published in the journal Nature Nanotechnology provides new insights into how skyrmions behave and how scientists can control them. The findings could help researchers develop smaller, more efficient computing systems and advance emerging quantum technologies.

Most of the research was conducted at the Institute of Physics at Johannes Gutenberg University in Mainz, Germany, with contributions from a professor at the Norwegian University of Science and Technology (NTNU).

"Skyrmions are small, magnetic vortices that can store information in a completely new way," explained Asle Sudbø, who leads NTNU's Centre for Quantum Spintronics (QuSpin).

Unlike conventional technologies, which require increasing amounts of space and energy to handle growing quantities of data, skyrmions could offer a much more efficient alternative.

"Instead of the current technology, where we constantly need more space and energy to process data, skyrmions could make it possible to build much smaller and more energy-efficient data storage systems," explained Sudbø.

Tiny Magnetic Vortices Could Enable Brain-Like Computers

The potential applications of skyrmions extend well beyond conventional data storage. They are also important to spintronics, which explores ways to use the magnetic properties of electrons in electronic devices, and to research involving topological quantum effects.

One particularly promising possibility is the development of computers that operate in ways similar to biological brains.

"These nanoscale vortices could help develop computers that resemble biological brains," said Sudbø.

Rather than relying on conventional transistor-based computing, such systems could use magnetism to process information. In principle, this approach could dramatically reduce electricity consumption, potentially allowing future computers to perform complex tasks with very little energy.

Turning that possibility into practical technology, however, requires scientists to understand exactly how skyrmions behave and how to manipulate them reliably.

The latest research represents an important advance toward that goal.

Scientists Film Magnetic Skyrmion Lattices Melting for the First Time

Skyrmions do not always exist as isolated magnetic structures. Under certain conditions, they organize themselves into repeating patterns known as lattices, resembling the orderly arrangement of atoms in a crystal, but on a much smaller scale.

Scientists can manipulate these arrangements, a capability that could be essential for using skyrmions in future computing devices.

In the new study, researchers achieved something that had not been demonstrated before. They recorded the melting of a magnetic skyrmion lattice as it happened while simultaneously controlling individual skyrmions.

"For the first time, researchers have managed to film how a magnetic skyrmion lattice melts in real time, while simultaneously being able to manipulate each individual skyrmion directly as they film it. For example, both their shape and size can be changed 'on the fly'," said Sudbø.

The ability to observe this process directly provides a clearer picture of how skyrmions respond when their organized structures break apart. It also allows researchers to study how changing the size and shape of individual magnetic vortices affects their behavior.

A New Step Toward Energy-Efficient Computing

Understanding how skyrmions behave during the breakdown of their lattices could help scientists develop more precise ways to control them. That control is particularly important if these magnetic structures are to become useful components in future computer systems.

The findings therefore offer more than a new way to observe an unusual magnetic phenomenon. They provide information that could help researchers overcome challenges involved in developing practical skyrmion-based technologies.

"The findings give us new insight into how these types of particles behave. This could therefore become important for the development of more energy-efficient computer technology in the future," said Sudbø.

Although substantial development is still needed before skyrmions can be used in everyday computing devices, the research highlights their potential for improving data storage, enabling new computing architectures, and supporting advances in quantum technology.

By learning to observe and manipulate these tiny magnetic vortices in real time, scientists are gaining capabilities that could eventually help reshape the design of computers.

"This could revolutionize the way we think about computers," concluded Sudbø.

Materials provided by Norwegian University of Science and Technology. Original written by Steinar Brandslet. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#456. Apple CarPlay and Android Auto Make Your Phone Hot. Here’s How to Keep It Cool

Android Auto and CarPlay have transformed the way people drive.

Rather than putting up with whatever infotainment systems car manufacturers have put together, we're now able to interact with our phones from our car dashboards—with our contacts, our music, our podcasts, and our mapping apps all available. And the list of supported vehicles just keeps growing.

Both the Google and Apple solutions for this are really simple to use too. Most of the time they'll connect up automatically once you've done the initial setup. What you might not realize, though, are the demands that Android Auto and CarPlay put on your phone. Your handset is essentially doing all the regular phone stuff and then powering a large tablet-like display at the same time. That typically includes constantly updating a map in real time.

With that in mind, your Android phone or iPhone can quickly start to lose battery life and heat up when it's connected to your vehicle, which you've perhaps already noticed. To keep your handset cool and your battery in good shape, here's what you need to do.

Wireless Android Auto and CarPlay are convenient, just like wireless charging is convenient, but it puts more stress on your phone. If you've got the option of a wired connection, that's preferable—but that introduces a new potential heat problem, which is that your phone will always be charging up.

To counter that, you can set charge limits (Battery and charging from Android Settings, Battery > Charging from iOS Settings), or simply make sure your phone is kept in a cool, dark spot of the car.

This wired option has the added bonus of letting you switch to airplane mode on your phone, which will go a long way to limiting heat. Again there's a trade-off though: No one will be able to reach you, and you'll need to have your maps, music, podcasts, and whatever else you need downloaded to your device in advance.

If you do stick with wireless Android Auto or CarPlay, try to avoid charging your phone at the same time, especially on the wireless pad built into the car’s dash (which generates more heat). You don't want to end up with a dead phone half way through the journey, so you may have to compromise at certain times.

As mentioned above, where your phone is in the car makes a real difference to how hot it's going to be. If you have your phone's interface up on your car dashboard, then you don't necessarily need to have it fixed to the dashboard, too, possibly under direct sunlight. The glove compartment will do just fine.

If you have a mount for your phone, think about attaching it in front of one of your car's air vents. Blasts of air conditioned air will significantly reduce how hot your handset gets, working like a DIY cooling system.

You might also want to think about taking the case off of your phone for the length of the trip, if your phone is fitted with one. Your handset is less likely to be dropped or knocked while you're driving anyway, and a case can trap the heat in.

The rules aren't too different to keeping your phone cool generally, though with the added wrinkle of needing to keep it connected to your car somehow. Make sure the cabin in general is at a comfortable temperature, though you're probably already doing that for your own sake. The bigger thing to remember is to avoid leaving your phone behind in a vehicle that's going to heat up in the sun if you're stopping off somewhere.

If your phone is hooked up to Android Auto or CarPlay, then it's probably got some work to do—whether it's getting you to a destination or streaming music. However, you can still reduce heat by keeping phone tasks down to a minimum. If your calls and (voice-activated) messages can wait, leave them until the end of the journey.

We mentioned offline syncing above, but it doesn't have to go along with airplane mode. If you sync your tunes, podcasts, and maps to your phone ahead of time, your handset has less work to do while you’re driving since it doesn’t need to pull data from the cloud while also constantly searching for a wireless signal as you travel. Less stress on the modem means less heat overall.

Both Apple Maps and Google Maps let you download maps ahead of a trip. In Apple Maps, tap your profile picture, then Offline Maps, then Add Offline Map and find your location. With Google Maps, tap your profile picture, then choose Offline maps > Select your own map to choose the coverage.

There may be times when your phone has to be charging, wirelessly connected, making calls, in direct sunlight, and streaming music over a cellular connection, but try to keep these times to a minimum. Check regularly on how hot your phone is getting while connected to Android Auto or CarPlay, so you can take action if needed.

The brand-new second-generation Apple AirTag just dropped to $23 at Amazon, down from its standard $29 sticker price. That shaves $6 right off the top, marking the lowest price we have ever tracked for Apple's upgraded tracker.

When Mashable's Stan Schroeder put it through its paces, the real-world tracking gains were immediately obvious. Grab the Apple AirTag (2nd Generation) while it's sitting at its all-time low.

#452. NASA’s Perseverance Offers Clues to Protect Crews From Mars Dust

Over 1,645 Martian days, NASA’s Perseverance Mars rover measured the fine and ultrafine Martian dust particles found clinging to samples of spacesuit materials that are carried aboard the rover, including some that are small enough to penetrate the lungs and enter the bloodstream. The findings will be published Tuesday, Oct. 27, in the Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences and could inform spacesuit and habitat design, dust-removal procedures, and crew health standards for future NASA missions to Mars.

“NASA has a long history of turning challenges into workable solutions and of finding creative ways to use the data that we have to answer questions beyond what it was originally collected for,” said Lindsay Hays, acting director, Planetary Science Division, NASA Headquarters in Washington. “Dust has been a surface-exploration challenge since the Apollo program. Perseverance is giving our engineers and flight surgeons measurements they can use to improve our existing designs well before the first American astronauts arrive on Mars.”

Lunar dust scratched visors, degraded outer spacesuit layers, and damaged their seals during NASA’s Apollo missions, and some crew members reported symptoms similar to hay fever.

Rounded by wind-driven collisions with other particles, dust grains on Mars are less abrasive than those on the Moon. They also are enriched in sulfur, chlorine, and iron and contain more silica than lunar dust. Regional and global dust storms on Mars can keep the grains recirculating throughout the atmosphere as well.

The rover carries a set of five samples of spacesuit material. The swatches, each three-quarters of an inch (20 millimeters) square, are part of a calibration target used to test the settings of SHERLOC (Scanning Habitable Environments with Raman and Luminescence for Organics and Chemicals), an instrument on Perseverance’s robotic arm. The calibration target for another instrument that provided the dust measurements, called PIXL (Planetary Instrument for X-ray Lithochemistry), carries a sample of Teflon and of basaltic glass.

Because Teflon contains no elements that PIXL can directly detect, whatever elemental signals the X-ray spectrometer picks up from that sample comes from dust resting on it.

The team also analyzed swatches of Teflon and Vectran, two materials used in spacesuits, on SHERLOC’s calibration target. NASA sent the swatches to Mars to study how spacesuit materials hold up on the planet’s surface.

“Perseverance’s main job is to search for signs of ancient microbial life, but the mission was also designed to help prepare for human explorers. That effort includes technology demonstrations like MOXIE (Mars Oxygen In-Situ Resource Utilization Experiment) and Ingenuity, as well as the spacesuit samples,” said Katie Stack Morgan, Perseverance’s project scientist at NASA’s Jet Propulsion Laboratory in Southern California.

While earlier measurements on Mars resolved dust particles down to about 3 micrometers across, PIXL detected two categories of even smaller particles: fine particles measuring less than 2.5 micrometers (a human hair is about 70 micrometers wide) and ultrafine particles around 100 nanometers (about 700 times thinner than a human hair). Particles on PIXL’s Teflon target and SHERLOC’s Vectran swatch averaged about 2 micrometers. On PIXL’s basaltic glass and SHERLOC’s Teflon swatch, dust particle sizes ranged down to about 100 to 300 nanometers.

Static electricity appears to determine which size dust particles stick to which surface. Dust grains become charged as they collide in the air, and different materials on the calibration target attract grains of different sizes. The finest particles stayed attached through years of driving and drilling. Organic polymers like SHERLOC’s Teflon spacesuit sample are common in the outer layers of spacesuits, and the results show they readily collect fine dust.

The study also found that some materials hold less dust than others, which could guide future choices of materials and protective coatings for spacesuits and habitats. Designs such as suitports, which let astronauts enter a suit attached to the outside of a habitat, and dual-chamber airlocks could reduce how much dust gets inside astronauts’ living quarters.

“The existence of Mars dust at these ultrafine sizes has been predicted for a long time, and PIXL gave us the chance to measure it directly on Mars,” said Scott VanBommel, a Perseverance scientist at Washington University in St. Louis and the paper’s lead author. “The larger clumps, the Martian dust bunnies, get shaken off over time. But the smallest particles stay behind, and those are the ones that pose the greatest challenge for human support systems.”

Chemically, the dust matches the iron- and sulfur-rich composition that earlier missions measured. The PIXL instrument also found the dust to be enriched in phosphorus and detected signs of calcium sulfate.

On Earth, airborne particles, or particulate matter, below 2.5 micrometers (that is, fine particles) are tied to a range of negative health effects because they can penetrate deep into the lungs and enter the bloodstream. Studies have shown that ultrafine particles (those no larger than 100 nanometers) can travel from the nasal cavity to the brain along the olfactory nerve.

Martian dust is composed predominantly of silicate minerals and carries chemically reactive compounds, including perchlorates that can disrupt thyroid function and reactive iron that induces cellular damage. Scientists believe that the health risks of Mars dust depend on the quantity of dust and length of exposure.

The PIXL results are the first on-site measurements of fine and ultrafine particles on Mars, and this research can inform broader efforts to refine indoor dust-exposure limits for astronauts, develop safe habits, and establish ways to remove dust while exploring Mars.

NASA is already working on solutions to extraterrestrial dust hazards, using ground test facilities and lunar missions as proving grounds for future Mars explorers. In March 2025, the agency tested an Electrodynamic Dust Shield, which used electric fields to clear lunar regolith from glass and thermal radiator surfaces on the Moon. Developed at NASA’s Kennedy Space Center in Florida, it landed on the Moon aboard Firefly Aerospace’s Blue Ghost Mission 1. At NASA’s Johnson Space Center in Houston, engineers at the Lunar Development and Test Facility use vacuum chambers and lunar regolith simulant to test spacesuit components, mechanisms, and a Handheld Lunar Electrostatic Dust Mitigation tool ahead of Artemis missions to the lunar surface.

Managed for NASA by Caltech, JPL built and manages operations of the Perseverance rover on behalf of the agency’s Science Mission Directorate as part of NASA’s Mars Exploration Program portfolio. NASA JPL also built PIXL and SHERLOC.

https://science.nasa.gov/mission/mars-2020-perseverance

#451. Russia arrests German man, calls him a Ukraine 'mercenary'

Russian FSB agents have arrested a German national and accused him of fighting as a mercenary for Ukraine's military. They claim he deserted over unpaid wages and say he was a sniper who once served in the Bundeswehr.

Russia's Federal Security Service (FSB) domestic intelligence agency said on Wednesday that it had arrested a German national on allegations of mercenary activity, calling him an "active member of the Ukrainian armed forces."

The news came on the same day that Russia released a retired teacher from the US, Stephen Hubbard, after years in detention on similar mercenary charges, which he denied.

Russia has detained a number of foreign nationals since its 2022 full-scale invasion of Ukraine, accusing them of assisting Kyiv in some way or another.

There was no immediate comment from Germany's government, besides acknowledging that it had seen the reports and was pursuing them.

The FSB said in a statement carried widely by Russian media that the man was a former Bundeswehr soldier and trained sniper. It did not issue his name but published a short video in which he could be heard speaking German.

According to the FSB, the man was a Bundeswehr soldier from 1996 to 2010, who served in Afghanistan and Somalia between 2004 and 2010.

They alleged that he later operated as a mercenary fighter in Syria's civil war.

From 2016 to 2018, according to the Russian spy agency, he fought in Ukraine against the Russian-backed rebels in eastern states, prior to Moscow's full-scale invasion in 2022.

The FSB claimed that he returned to Ukraine as a "mercenary" in 2025 and was fighting in the contested region of Donetsk, but then deserted over non-payment of wages.

They did not comment on why or how the man, whom they said was born in 1976, had come to be in Russia. Nor did they say where or under what circumstances they arrested him.

A spokesperson at the German Foreign Ministry only initially responded by saying that Berlin was aware of the reports from Russia.

The spokesperson said the ministry would not comment on the individual case or specifics for the time being.

Russia has arrested hundreds of foreign nationals on charges like espionage, sabotage and treason amid its invasion of neighboring Ukraine. The charges can carry lengthy prison sentences.

A smaller number have also been accused of mercenary activity.

The FSB last week arrested a Swedish national, again accusing him of fighting on Kyiv's behalf.

In his case, they said he was operating as the commander of a drone unit and that he was detained attempting to illegally cross the border in the western Pskov region.

A Japanese journalist was also arrested last week, accused of espionage, prompting sharp criticism from Tokyo.

Also on Wednesday, Russia released a retired US teacher, Stephen Hubbard, who had been arrested in 2024 on comparable allegations.

This took place as US negotiator Steve Witkoff was visiting St. Petersburg and after a series of recent concessions to Russia and criticisms of Ukraine from US President Donald Trump.

Trump claimed there was no quid pro quo involved in the release, but later said Hubbard would not be free if he had not eased sanctions on Russian diesel shipments.

#450. 1.9-million-year-old discovery rewrites the story of human evolution

An archaeological site in the Jordan Valley is rewriting the timeline of early human migration. New research shows that 'Ubeidiya is at least 1.9 million years old, placing humans in the region hundreds of thousands of years earlier than previously believed. The revised dating makes 'Ubeidiya, alongside Dmanisi in Georgia, one of the oldest known sites of early human activity outside Africa. The findings suggest that ancient human populations, using different types of stone tools, had already spread into the Levant during a crucial early period of human expansion beyond Africa.

The research was led by Prof. Ari Matmon of the Hebrew University of Jerusalem, Prof. Omry Barzilai of the University of Haifa, and Prof. Miriam Belmaker of the University of Tulsa. By combining three advanced dating methods, the team established a more reliable timeline for 'Ubeidiya, a site that has played an important role in research on human evolution.

Their findings indicate that the site is at least 1.9 million years old, making it one of the earliest known locations where ancient humans lived outside their African homeland.

Ancient Stone Tools Reveal Early Human Activity

The 'Ubeidiya Formation has attracted scientific attention for decades because of its early evidence of the Acheulean stone tool tradition. This technology is known for large tools shaped on both sides, including hand axes. The site also contains a rich collection of animal fossils, representing species originally associated with Africa and Asia, including several that are now extinct.

Despite its importance, researchers have struggled to establish exactly when early humans occupied the area. Previous estimates generally placed 'Ubeidiya between 1.2 million and 1.6 million years old, but those dates relied on comparisons with other archaeological and geological evidence rather than direct measurements.

To resolve the uncertainty, the researchers collected new samples from the site and analyzed them using three complementary dating techniques. Each provided an independent source of evidence about the age of the ancient deposits.

The first technique, called cosmogenic isotope burial dating, uses rare isotopes that form when cosmic rays interact with rocks exposed at Earth's surface. After the rocks become buried, these isotopes decay at known rates. Measuring what remains allows scientists to estimate how long the rocks have been underground.

The researchers also investigated evidence of Earth's ancient magnetic field recorded in sediments deposited at the bottom of a lake. As these sediments accumulated, they preserved the orientation of the magnetic field at the time. Comparing these records with known magnetic reversals showed that the deposits formed during the Matuyama Chron, a period that began more than two million years ago.

A third technique involved fossilized shells from Melanopsis, a genus of freshwater snails found within the sediment. Using uranium-lead dating, the team was able to establish a minimum age for the geological layers containing the ancient stone tools.

Together, the three approaches pointed to a much older age than earlier estimates.

A 1.9-Million-Year-Old Discovery Changes the Human Migration Timeline

The combined evidence indicates that 'Ubeidiya dates back at least 1.9 million years, significantly changing the timeline of early human settlement beyond Africa.

This places the Jordan Valley site at approximately the same age as Dmanisi in Georgia, another major location for understanding early human migration. The similarity in age suggests that ancient human populations were expanding into different regions around the same time.

The findings also raise intriguing questions about how these early migrations unfolded. Evidence from the two sites suggests that different groups of ancient humans may have carried distinct stone tool technologies out of Africa during overlapping periods.

These included the Oldowan tradition, associated with relatively simple stone tools, and the more sophisticated Acheulean tradition, known for carefully shaped tools such as hand axes. Rather than spreading in a single technological sequence, these different approaches to toolmaking may have traveled beyond Africa at roughly the same time as separate groups of early humans entered new environments.

Solving a Three-Million-Year-Old Dating Puzzle

The researchers also had to explain an unexpected complication in their results. Initial measurements of cosmogenic isotopes suggested that some rocks were approximately three million years old, considerably older than indicated by the site's magnetic, fossil, geological, and archaeological evidence.

Further investigation revealed why the measurements appeared to conflict.

The sediments had undergone a complicated history of repeated deposition, burial, exposure, and movement within the Dead Sea rift and the surrounding landscape. Some of the material had been buried long before it was eventually transported and deposited again near the ancient lake at 'Ubeidiya.

This recycling meant that the geological history recorded by certain rocks extended much farther into the past than the formation of the archaeological layers themselves.

"The exposure-burial history that emerges from the model implies recycling of sediments previously deposited and buried in the rift valley... and then redeposited along the 'Ubeidiya paleo lake shoreline."

By accounting for this complex sediment history, the team reconciled the conflicting measurements and strengthened the evidence for the site's revised age.

The results establish 'Ubeidiya as one of the oldest known centers of early human activity outside Africa. They also suggest that the movement of our ancient relatives into new territories was already underway 1.9 million years ago, potentially involving multiple populations with different toolmaking traditions.

Materials provided by The Hebrew University of Jerusalem. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#449. Southwest Airlines cuts 2026 profit forecast on higher fuel

Southwest Airlines became the third major U.S. airline this month to cut its 2026 profit forecast due to higher fuel prices, but CEO Bob Jordan said travelers continue to book despite higher fares.

The carrier expects to earn between $2.60 and $2.85 a share on an adjusted basis this year, down from its forecast in July of between $3.25 to as much as $4.25 a share.

Here's what Southwest reported for third quarter compared with Wall Street expectations, according to consensus estimates from LSEG:

Southwest forecast per-share adjusted earnings of between 55 cents and 80 cents in the fourth quarter and unit revenue, a gauge of airfare, up as much as 21% from a year earlier. It said it expects to grow capacity by up to 4% year over year.

"Demand remained strong as we lapped last year's bag fee and other commercial initiatives, reinforcing our belief that the earnings power we've built is durable, even with significantly higher fuel costs," Jordan said in an earnings release.

The airline said its average passenger fare was $231.69 in the third quarter, up almost 27% from the same quarter of 2025. A spokesman said that number does not include add-on fees. The number of revenue passengers carried fell 3.2% to 33.47 million people.

But Jordan told CNBC's Phil LeBeau that demand was strong across every geography and product.

"The consumer is incredibly strong and we do not see that abating," Jordan said. "We're seeing strength in both the holiday period and the non-holiday period."

Under pressure to grow revenue, Southwest has been remaking its longtime business model over the past several years, scrapping its old policies like two free checked bags per passenger and open seating. In its place, Southwest has started charging for many seat assignments and added a bag fee, similar to major competitors.

For the third quarter, Southwest posted net income of $270 million, or 55 cents a share, up from $54 million or 10 cents a share a year earlier. Revenue rose more than 21% to $8.42 billion.

Adjusting for one-time items, Southwest reported 66 cents a share.

Delta Air Lines and United Airlines also cut their profit outlooks for the rest of the year when they reported third-quarter earnings. Both of those airlines reported top- and bottom-line misses for the third quarter.

#447. Oil Spills Wreak Havoc on the Bottom of the Ocean, Even a Decade Later

This story originally appeared on Inside Climate News and is part of the Climate Desk collaboration.

Across hundreds of miles of the northern Gulf of Mexico, a deepwater ecosystem collapsed after the massive 2011 Deepwater Horizon oil and gas spill, and recovery is uncertain, new research concludes.

The study, published Monday in the Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences, found that animal populations—including octopus, krill, squid, and shrimp—declined by 60 to 85 percent in the three and a half years after the spill, with few signs of recovery since. Krill and lanternfish, both foundational species in the Gulf’s food web, were among the hardest hit. The findings also suggest that the die-off is causing genetic bottlenecks that could further hinder ecosystem recovery.

The researchers identified it as the “fastest and most severe faunal decline reported for Earth’s largest ecosystem type” ever documented. The widespread die-off of marine organisms may also reduce the ocean’s ability to transport carbon dioxide from the atmosphere to the deep sea. After ruling out other factors, such as water temperature and oxygen levels, the researchers identified the Deepwater Horizon blowout as “the most parsimonious explanation” for the ecosystem’s collapse.

Comprehensive natural resource damage assessments and restoration efforts involving federal, state, and tribal trustees are ongoing. In 2016, BP agreed to a $20.8 billion settlement, including up to $8.8 billion for environmental restoration.

Remote ocean depths and habitats miles below the surface may seem buffered from the impacts of human activity, the authors wrote, but their findings suggest an industrial disaster pushed part of it into a diminished state with “limited resilience to facilitate recovery.” They said their findings also show that proposed industrial deep-sea mining could damage the ecosystem before baseline conditions are assessed.

A transoceanic drilling rig, operating for BP about 50 miles southeast of the Mississippi Delta, exploded on April 20, 2010. The explosion and fire killed 11 people and triggered the largest accidental marine oil spill in history.

Oil and gas spewed uncontrollably from the broken well nearly a mile deep for 87 days, ejecting 4.9 million barrels of oil at a rate of about 400 tanker trucks per day, enough to fill up 1.2 million cars. The spill also released about 7.7 billion cubic feet of fossil gas into the deep sea at a rate of 88 million cubic feet per day, enough to supply about 820,000 US homes. State and federal agencies, including the Environmental Protection Agency, also authorized BP to apply 1.84 million gallons of chemical dispersants to try to reduce the impacts.

Some of the oil and gas dissipated and was transported out of the deep Gulf of Mexico region. Some coated barrier islands and devastated coastal wetlands and shellfish habitat, and some settled onto the ocean floor, where currents can stir it back into the water. But enough hydrocarbon and chemical-dispersant remnants stayed suspended long enough to do almost immeasurable harm.

A series of studies since the spill have shown a wide range of toxic effects on marine organisms, from impaired development and reproduction to increased mortality and lasting health problems in dolphins and whales. The new paper suggests that lingering pollutants may interfere with some species’ ability to find mates in the dark by disrupting their highly developed sense of smell.

The impacts continue to ripple through the ecosystem, said lead author Tracey Sutton, a professor of marine and environmental sciences at Nova Southeastern University in Florida. A 2024 study found an 80 percent decline in deep-diving toothed whales, he said.

“We used to have a big resident population of sperm whales right off the Mississippi River, but we haven’t seen a sperm whale in 10 years,” he said. Large predators need big, occasional dense patches of prey, and those have disappeared, he added.

Scientists have developed accurate ways to count fish. For this study, they started with a baseline from nine months of sampling in 2011 and compared it with nine subsequent surveys between 2015 and 2023. The researchers sampled deep-pelagic habitat along a large grid around the spill site from about 650 feet below the surface to about a mile down. The deep-pelagic zone accounts for about 90 percent of the Gulf’s total volume.

Across a grid of stations, scientists on slippery decks lowered and hoisted nets that open and close at different depths, with experts from different species groups to identify the catch. They sampled around noon and midnight to account for species that migrate vertically each day. Ship-mounted scientific echosounders provided an independent check, measuring sound reflected by marine life in the water column.

The research is “comprehensive and carefully composed,” marine biologist Bruce Robison, a senior scientist at the Monterey Bay Aquarium Research Institute, said via email. “There is no other study that I know of with such an incredible number of net samples,” added Robison, who did not participate in the new study.

The research focuses on species that are not commercially fished and shows for the first time that human impacts can deplete marine life even in very deep ocean water, he said.

“It is important for the public to understand just how disastrous this oil spill was,” Robison said. “The Gulf has not recovered, and it’s been over 15 years.” Robison said other activities, such as deep-sea mining, could also have severe negative consequences for deepwater pelagic ecosystems by discharging wastewater containing metals or lubricants.

Three lines of evidence—net hauls, acoustic surveys, and genetic sampling—corroborate each other, which is “unusually strong evidence to support his conclusions,” said Jeffrey Drazen, a marine ecologist at the University of Hawaii at Manoa, who did not participate in the new study.

The study examines all possible causes of the decline and shows only one aligns with the scientific evidence, he said.

“Through logical elimination,” he said, “and without actually pointing a finger, they leave no doubt that the oil spill is the cause of the decline.”

#446. November full moon 2026: When, where and how to see the Beaver Moon
