Your guide to November's full Beaver Moon, from peak times to skywatching highlights.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The November full moon reaches maximum illumination at 9:53 a.m. EST (1453 GMT) on Nov. 24, as the lunar disk sits opposite the sun in Earth's sky, appearing fully lit by its rays.

November's full moon is traditionally known as the "Beaver Moon", because it rises around the time when the small mammals take refuge in their dens, having stored enough food to survive the long winter months, according to the Old Farmer's Almanac.

The Nov. 24 full moon also happens to be the first of three consecutive "supermoons". The non-scientific term is often bestowed on full moons that occur when the natural satellite is within 90% of perigee, when the moon is closest to Earth in its elliptical orbit. The proximity can make a supermoon appear 14% larger and 30% brighter than the smallest full moon of the year, according to NASA.

The November full moon will be below the horizon for stargazers in the U.S. when it turns full at 9:53 a.m. EST on Nov. 24. Instead, viewers in America will get their best view of the near-full Beaver Moon as it arcs towards the western horizon in the predawn hours of Nov. 23.

You may notice the lunar disk take on a gorgeous yellow-orange hue in the hour before moonset, as the bluer wavelengths of its light are scattered by Earth's dense atmosphere through a process known as Rayleigh scattering — the same phenomenon that turns the sky red at sunset.

November's supermoon may appear even larger as it travels close to the horizon thanks to a phenomenon known as the "Moon Illusion", which tricks us into thinking that the lunar disk is larger than it actually is while closer to foreground objects, compared to when it shines overhead. The moon will also appear fully lit to the casual observer on the evenings of Nov. 23 and Nov. 24, so there's no need to miss out if you struggle with early mornings!

The table below details local full moon timings for when the moon is 100% illuminated for a selection of cities spread across the globe (local moonset and moonrise times will vary based on your location):

The November full moon is often called the Beaver Moon because it rises around the time that beavers retreat to their lodges after collecting enough food to see them through the winter.

This is a fantastic go-to telescope with a 4-inch aperture that can help you quickly find a steady view of the moon. For a closer look at craters and the terminator, make sure to grab a 9mm or 12mm eyepiece and get stronger magnification.

It is also known as the Frost Moon by the indigenous Cree people of Canada and the northwestern U.S., and as the Digging Moon by the Tlingit, in reference to the animals that scratch around for fallen nuts in the hardening autumn earth, per the Old Farmer's Almanac.

The rising of the November full moon also marks the Hindu festival of Dev Diwali when worshippers light earthen lamps called "diyas" and offer prayers to celebrate Lord Shiva's defeat of the demon Tripurasura, after which the Hindu gods descended to Earth to celebrate, according to the Edinburgh Hindu Mandir & Cultural Centre.

Nov. 24 and the rising of the full moon also coincides with the Sikh celebration of Guru Nanak Gurpurab, when millions celebrate the birthday of Guru Nanak, the founder of Sikhism, according to the BBC.

Stargazers in the U.S. will be treated to a magnificent natural light show in the predawn hours of Nov. 24, when the near-full moon shines low on the western horizon less than 5 degrees from the bright stars of the Pleiades open star cluster.

The Pleiades and moon will be close enough to share the field of view of a pair of 10X50 binoculars, which will help reveal gorgeous detail on the lunar surface, while helping to resolve dozens more of the clusters blue-white stars as they cut through the deluge of moonlight.

Tear your gaze away from the moon and find a trio of spectacular multi-colored lights forming a tight triangle high in the southern sky. The red light of Mars will shine steadily above the twinkling star Regulus at the heart of the constellation Leo, with the yellow-white light of Jupiter to their right.

Venus will also be visible as a dazzling "Morning Star" hanging low on the eastern horizon, with Mercury below, tracing out the path of the approaching sun.

Saturn, meanwhile, will make for a satisfyingly easy and visually spectacular target in the post sunset hours of Nov. 24, as it shines in the southeastern sky in the constellation Pisces, whose stars will be a challenge to spot in the light of the newly waning moon.

The November full moon presents a stunning opportunity to photograph the entirety of the Earth-facing lunar disk as its disk appears temporarily free from shadow. Be sure to make use of our expert guide to photographing the moon, along with our picks of the best cameras and lenses for astrophotography, if you want to upgrade your equipment.

8:28 p.m. EST on Dec. 23 (0128 GMT on Dec. 24)

7:17 a.m. EST (1217 GMT) on Jan. 22, 2027

5:09 a.m. EST (1009 GMT) on Feb. 20, 2027

These times mark the exact instant of full illumination. Your own moonrise or moonset may be hours earlier or later, depending on your location. For the best viewing plan, use an app such as Stellarium or SkySafari 7 Pro to find your local moonrise time and be sure to read our full moon calendar for comprehensive timings for each full moon in 2026.

#445. Micron Technology: China probes US chip maker for cybersecurity risks as tech tension escalates

China has launched a cybersecurity probe into Micron Technology, one of America’s largest memory chip makers, in apparent retaliation after US allies in Asia and Europe announced new restrictions on the sale of key technology to Beijing.

The Cyberspace Administration of China (CAC) will review products sold by Micron in the country, according to a statement by the watchdog late on Friday.

The move is aimed at “ensuring the security of key information infrastructure supply chains, preventing cybersecurity risks caused by hidden product problems, and maintaining national security,” it noted.

It came on the same day that Japan, a US ally, said it would restrict the export of advanced chip manufacturing equipment to countries including China, following similar moves by the United States and the Netherlands.

Washington and its allies have announced curbs on China’s semiconductor industry, which strike at the heart of Beijing’s bid to become a tech superpower.

Last month, the Netherlands also unveiled new restrictions on overseas sales of semiconductor technology, citing the need to protect national security. In October, the United States banned Chinese companies from buying advanced chips and chipmaking equipment without a license.

Micron told CNN it was aware of the review.

“We are in communication with the CAC and are cooperating fully,” it said, adding that it stands by the security of its products. “Micron’s product shipments, engineering, manufacturing, sales and other functions are operating as normal.”

Shares in Micron sank 4.4% on Wall Street Friday following the news, the biggest drop in more than three months. On Monday, they closed another 1.2% lower. Micron derives more than 10% of its revenue from China.

In an earlier filing, the Idaho-based company had warned of such risks.

“The Chinese government may restrict us from participating in the China market or may prevent us from competing effectively with Chinese companies,” it said last week.

Japan joins the US and Europe in chipmaking curbs on China

China has strongly criticized restrictions on tech exports, saying last month it “firmly opposes” such measures.

In efforts to boost growth and job creation, Beijing is seeking to woo foreign investments as it grapples with mounting economic challenges. The newly minted premier Li Qiang and several top economic officials have been rolling out the welcome wagon for global CEOs and promising they would “provide a good environment and services.”

But Beijing has also exerted growing pressure on foreign companies to bring them into line with its agenda.

Last month, authorities closed the Beijing office of Mintz Group, a US corporate intelligence firm, and detained five local staff.

Days earlier, they suspended Deloitte’s operations in Beijing for three months and imposed a fine of $31 million over alleged lapses in its work auditing a state-owned distressed debt manager.

#441. Raids at far-right group's offices at European Parliament

Authorities searched the Patriots for Europe's European Parliament offices in a long-running funding inquiry. The group says the charges pertain to its predecessor and there is "no legal continuity" between the two.

The third-largest alliance of parties in the European Parliament, the right-wing nationalist Patriots for Europe (PfE) group, on Wednesday said searches had been carried out at "administrative offices" at the European Parliament buildings in Brussels and Strasbourg.

PfE said the searches appeared to be tied to the long-running probe into alleged misuse of funds by members of the now-disbanded Identity and Democracy (ID) Group.

PfE formed out of the ashes of ID, losing and gaining some members along the way, after the last European Parliament elections in 2024.

The European Public Prosecutor's Office — the independent body that investigates and prosecutes crimes against the EU budget, such as corruption or misuse of EU funds — said it searched premises in France and Belgium.

It said this was part of an ongoing investigation into the use of European Union funds by a former European Parliament group.

EPPO did not say which group was involved and said it would not publish further details for now for fear of compromising the investigation.

PfE, meanwhile, confirmed the activity around its offices but also tried to stress its distance from the case since its rebrand and reformation — even though its constituent political parties are largely the same with the notable exception of the Alternative for Germany (AfD) now being excluded.

"Since this morning, the European Public Prosecutor's Office has been carrying out searches in administrative offices at the European Parliament in Brussels and Strasbourg. It would appear that these searches are linked to the complaint lodged against the Identity and Democracy Group (ID) dissolved in 2024," it wrote in a statement.

"The Patriots for Europe Group emphasizes that there is no legal continuity with the ID Group and that it is not involved in the ongoing investigations," it said.

A French member of the European Parliament for the Rassemblement National or National Rally (RN) party, Jean-Paul Garraud, said that "the offices of staff members of the former Identity and Democracy group" were targeted.

European Parliament auditors concluded last year that ID members had misused at least €4.3 million (roughly $4.8 million) in EU funds during the previous legislative term from 2019 to 2024.

According to their report, the money flowed to companies close to the group and to associations supporting anti-abortion campaigners.

The case was reminiscent of, but separate from, the conviction against RN leader Marine Le Pen, herself a former MEP, that for a time looked set to stifle her bid for the French presidency.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

The ID group existed for one term in the European Parliament, from 2019 to 2024. It included France's RN, Germany's AfD, Austria's Freedom Party (FPÖ) and Italy's Lega, among other members.

It fell apart after major internal friction during the 2024 European election campaign, not least because of dissatisfaction within the group with their lead candidate Maximilian Krah of the AfD over a number of issues — from controversial remarks about Nazi Germany through to allegations of his close allies being Russian and Chinese influence, with one of Krah's aides since convicted of spying for China.

RN, FPÖ and Lega are part of the new PfE group, which claims "no legal continuity" to the dissolved ID alliance, but the AfD was not invited to join the PfE.

PfE has 85 seats in the 719-seat European Parliament, making it the third-largest group in the chamber, but a rather distant third behind the center-right European People's Party (184) and the center-left Socialists and Democrats (135).

The European Parliament relies heavily on such groupings, which try to unite political parties from various member states with broadly comparable policy positions and political alignment.

The AfD set up a much smaller splinter far-right grouping called Europe of Sovereign Nations (ESN), with just 27 seats, 15 of them held by the AfD.

#439. Mayo Clinic discovers new ways to fight the “zombie” cells that drive aging

Seven studies from Mayo Clinic are revealing new details about senescent cells, often called "zombie" cells because they stop dividing but remain active in the body. These aging cells release inflammatory substances that can contribute to diseases associated with growing older.

The research, published in Nature journals, was conducted by scientists at Mayo Clinic's Robert and Arlene Kogod Center on Aging. Rather than simply confirming that senescent cells play a role in aging, the studies investigate how to recognize different types of these cells, why they produce harmful inflammation and how their effects might be reduced.

The findings also contribute to a nationwide effort to create detailed maps of senescent cells throughout the human body. Known as the Cellular Senescence Network (SenNet) Consortium, the project brings together researchers across the United States and receives funding from the National Institutes of Health Common Fund. Its goal is to document where senescent cells occur in different tissues and how they change throughout a person's lifetime.

"This body of work is moving the aging field forward to precisely identify, understand and target the specific senescent cells that contribute to age-related dysfunction, which is the necessary groundwork to develop new therapies that support healthy aging," says Marissa Schafer, Ph.D., a Mayo Clinic scientist whose laboratory directed several of the investigations.

Scientists Discover That Zombie Cells Are Not All Alike

Identifying senescent cells reliably has long been a major obstacle for scientists studying aging. The new research shows that these cells are much more varied than previously understood. Their characteristics differ depending on where they are located in the body and which biological processes caused them to become senescent.

To help address this challenge, researchers developed a tool called the SenMayo gene set. It uses patterns of gene activity to identify senescent cells more consistently across tissues and experimental systems.

Scientists tested the approach using bone biopsies from older adults. They also found that the gene signature became less prominent after senescent cells were removed in preclinical experiments. Since its development, SenMayo has become a widely used resource for researchers investigating aging and related diseases.

Mayo Clinic endocrinologist Sundeep Khosla, M.D., served as senior author of the study, "A new gene set identifies senescent cells and predicts senescence-associated pathways across tissues." Other Mayo Clinic researchers who contributed to the paper include Nathan LeBrasseur, Ph.D., Diana Jurk, Ph.D., João Passos, Ph.D., and LaTonya Hickson, M.D..

A Hidden Connection Between Mitochondria and Chronic Inflammation

Other studies explored one of the most damaging features of zombie cells: their tendency to generate ongoing inflammation.

The researchers discovered additional biological processes that explain why senescent cells continue producing inflammatory signals. Several of these processes involve malfunctioning mitochondria, the tiny structures responsible for producing much of a cell's energy.

In one pathway, DNA and RNA originating from mitochondria activate immune responses that contribute to inflammation. Another involves changes in mitochondrial metabolism that influence inflammatory gene activity through epigenetic mechanisms. These mechanisms affect how genes are switched on or off without changing the underlying DNA sequence.

The discoveries suggest that researchers may eventually be able to interfere with the processes responsible for chronic inflammation while leaving the senescent cells themselves intact. This would offer another approach to treatment beyond attempting to destroy every harmful aging cell.

Dr. Passos served as senior author of three papers examining these mechanisms and the diversity of senescent cells:

Experimental Treatments Show Promise Against Aging Cells

The Mayo Clinic team also investigated possible ways to reduce the number of senescent cells or limit the damage they cause.

The approaches included senolytic medications, which are designed to eliminate senescent cells, a natural compound present in tomatoes, and genetic techniques intended to target particular populations of aging cells.

When tested in older mice, these interventions produced encouraging results. Depending on the approach, researchers observed reductions in inflammatory measures and improvements in several aging-related outcomes, including physical frailty, inflammation in the brain and performance on cognitive tasks.

Another investigation uncovered a possible way to measure aspects of biological aging through blood testing. Researchers identified IL-23R as a potential biomarker that might eventually help scientists evaluate aging-related changes and determine whether future treatments are working.

These results remain preliminary in terms of medical applications. More research will be necessary to establish whether the approaches are safe and effective in people. Nevertheless, the experiments provide valuable clues for developing treatments that more precisely address the cellular processes involved in age-related diseases.

Dr. Schafer was the lead author of two studies examining biomarkers and possible interventions:

A New Direction for Healthy Aging Research

Together, the seven studies represent an important shift in how researchers approach cellular aging. Scientists are moving beyond the broad observation that senescent cells contribute to age-related decline and toward identifying which populations cause the greatest problems, what makes them harmful and how those specific effects could be controlled.

The findings also challenge the idea that eliminating senescent cells must always be the primary treatment strategy. By understanding the molecular pathways that trigger persistent inflammation, researchers may be able to develop therapies that block damaging signals without removing the cells responsible for producing them.

Mayo Clinic researcher Darren Baker, Ph.D., served as senior author of the paper "Senotypes define the diverse landscape of senescent cells," which examines the varied characteristics of these aging cell populations.

The work also supports Mayo Clinic's broader Precure Research initiative. This program aims to equip physicians with better ways to identify and intervene in harmful biological changes before they develop into disease or advance into conditions that are difficult to treat.

By revealing how zombie cells differ, why they promote inflammation and which processes might be targeted, the research offers a more detailed roadmap for future treatments designed to support healthier aging.

#438. The MacBook Pro Max Just Dropped to Its Lowest Price Ever

An exciting new MacBook is rumored to be announced in just one week. If the reports end up being true, it'll be the most significant change to the lineup in many years, introducing a touchscreen OLED MacBook Pro. As exciting as it sounds, however, it's also the perfect opportunity to buy up the previous generation's M5 Max MacBook Pro at its lowest price ever.

That's especially significant considering the entire lineup got a price increase back in June. Right now, the 16-inch M5 Max MacBook Pro with 36 GB of RAM and 2 TB of storage is $3,499, which is 20 percent off the retail price and $400 cheaper than it was just a few days ago. And historically, this is $150 cheaper than this configuration has ever sold for.

This absolutely stacked version of the MacBook Pro is one of the most powerful laptops you can buy. The M5 Max is a huge upgrade over both the M4 Max and the M5 Pro, offering GPU performance we've never seen on a MacBook before. For content creators, media professionals, engineers, and AI enthusiasts, this is nearly as good as it gets. It can even play AAA games smoother than any Mac (outside of the new Mac Studio). In my testing, Cyberpunk 2077 could average over 60 frame per second on the Ultra preset without relying on upscaling.

If you really want to get serious about AI performance, you'll want to upgrade to the 64-GB or 128-GB configurations—though third-party retailers like Amazon don't sell them. You'll have to buy directly from Apple for that, and the prices are exorbitant. But the 36-GB model that's on sale has plenty of juice for dipping your toe in the world of on-device AI, and it's a home run for video editors, designers, musicians, and engineers.

Beyond performance, it goes without saying that all MacBook Pros have the best speakers, displays, build quality, trackpads, and keyboards of just about any laptop. I test dozens of new laptops every year, and while some have come close (such as the Surface Laptop Ultra I just finished testing), none have taken the crown.

You might be wondering if waiting to see these new MacBooks next week is worth it. Maybe. I can't guarantee that this deal will stick around, and I don't know what these new MacBooks will be like. Rumors say that they will sport the same chip options as the current MacBook Pros, meaning we'd get an M5 Pro and M5 Max once again. So if you're mostly interested in the performance, you can save some serious cash by snagging this M5 Max deal.

#437. David's Bridal plans to spin off AI platform Pearl into publicly traded company

David's Bridal is planning to spin off its artificial intelligence platform, Pearl, into a stand-alone publicly traded company targeting a valuation of roughly $350 million, CNBC learned exclusively.

The company expects the deal to close in the first half of 2027.

David's Bridal filed a nonbinding letter of intent on Thursday to separate out Pearl. The AI-centered business would own David's Bridal's intellectual property and look to build on its growing AI model, while the original company would try to strengthen its physical retail business.

"We have a fast-growing, high-margin business generating positive cash flow right now in Pearl, and going public now will help us attract the talent and give us more optionality to fuel the growth that we believe that we have," David's Bridal CEO Kelly Cook, who will take over as CEO of Pearl after the deal closes, told CNBC in an interview.

Cook said the companies will work hand in hand to serve customers throughout the wedding-planning process, adding that the split is "positioning both companies for strategic growth."

Pearl's services include an agentic AI-powered wedding-planning platform, a proprietary tool to streamline appointments and communications and a digital content network with original programming. It uses AI to personalize its suggestions based on its data collection for each client.

David's Bridal expects Pearl to generate an EBITDA margin of more than 30% this year and is projecting revenue growth of more than 100% in 2027, exceeding $85 million. It's expected to be debt-free after the deal closes and have positive free cash flow over the next two years, according to the company.

"Pearl has earned the right to stand on its own because it's created more value than we've expected," Cook said.

Following the transaction, Pearl will own David's Bridal's intellectual property and license it back to the chain.

"It's a very healthy business model," Cook said.

She added that the demand remains strong for both of the businesses. Grand View Research estimates the U.S. wedding services market will top $96 billion in 2026 — and both Pearl and David's Bridal want to be at the forefront of that segment.

The companies will continue to have a "relationship built on reciprocity," she said, sharing proprietary data and pointing customers to the other business for the next steps in their wedding planning. The split will allow each company to refine its focus, she added.

Still, the plan to go public comes at a time when AI stocks have been struggling. The initial public offering market for consumer companies has been lackluster, more companies are postponing their public debuts and investors are beginning to lose confidence in the AI boom.

Cook said the differentiator for Pearl is that the company is "not AI for AI's sake."

"Most AI companies have to find a customer," she said. "We already have her ... we have her information, and it's less about the AI side and more about modernizing this entire ecosystem for her that desperately needed modernization."

David's Bridal is no stranger to financial struggles. The company filed for bankruptcy protection in 2018 and emerged a few months later. It then filed a second time in 2023, citing a significant business slowdown from the pandemic and high inflation. To save itself, the company then sold its business to asset manager Cion Investment Corp. to avoid a shutdown.

As part of the spinoff deal, Cion has proposed to invest $25 million as an anchor investor in the separate company.

Cook, who took over as CEO last year to help it transition from a legacy retailer to a more cohesive online and in-store experience, said the company has "been through a hard chapter," but those struggles led to the decision to split the business.

"That's no secret, we've talked about that openly," she said. "What's extraordinary is what came out of that process is a leaner David's, a more focused David's ... I think it's a move from strength."

#434. Astronomers discover the secrets of 'string of pearls' supernova relic and the zombie star at its heart

Join The Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Latest space missions, discoveries and exploration updates.

Stargazing tips, cosmic events and expert insights on the universe

Space.com's Sci-Fi Reader's Club. Read a sci-fi short story every month and join a virtual community of fellow science fiction fans!

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

"The planetary region of our solar system could fit in each knot about ten times with room to spare."

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Scientists are still learning about a cosmic "string of pearls," a unique celestial relic left by a supernova explosion that humanity witnessed around 850 years ago.

That is thanks to the most detailed observation of the dandelion-shaped chains of gas and supernova debris that comprise Pa 30, the only supernova remnant of its type observed in the Milky Way. What makes Pa 30 even more unique is that it has a rare "zombie star" at its heart.

Supernovas like the one that created Pa 30 are called Type Ia supernovas; they happen when a dead star called a white dwarf overfeeds on a companion star like a cosmic vampire. This overfeeding triggers a runaway nuclear explosion that usually destroys the white dwarf. But on rare occasions, the supernova doesn't completely destroy the star, and a shattered stellar body remains, called a zombie star. These events are called Type Iax supernovas.

Pa 30 was first identified in 2013 by a citizen scientist in 2013, but scientists soon linked it to a cosmic explosion recorded independently in the year 1181 in Japanese, Chinese, and Arabic historical documentation. That makes this one of only five supernovas seen before the invention of the telescope.

"Reliable historical records of stellar explosions extend back only about a thousand years. For all we know, there might well be similar remnants still lurking in the dark in our own galaxy," team member Ilaria Caiazzo, assistant professor at the Institute of Science and Technology Austria (ISTA), said in a statement.

"Today, we are lucky to study an intriguing object we know was observed by astronomers who made three independent historical records of the event centuries before the invention of the telescope."

Pa 30 is a good laboratory to study supernovas because, at around 7,500 light-years away, it is relatively close to Earth on cosmic scales. For instance, the heart of our galaxy is around 26,000 light-years away. "We can see this much detail because of Pa 30's proximity to Earth," said Caiazzo. "This proximity makes Pa 30 uniquely valuable for study."

It is also an important subject of study because it is the only supernova remnant in which we can see a zombie star.

Using the Gemini North telescope, Caiazzo and colleagues zoomed in on Pa 30, discovering knots along the strands of gas radiating out from the site of the cosmic explosion, like pearls on a string.

These pearls are each wide enough to encompass the orbit of Neptune, the most distant planet from the sun, ten times over.

"The planetary region of our solar system could fit in each knot about ten times with room to spare," said Caiazzo. "The knots are quite strikingly uniform. We are excited to try to model them."

The team also discovered that the zombie star sits almost perfectly at the heart of Pa 30. When these cosmic explosions happen, they should provide any debris, including zombie stars, with a kick. The fact that this "living dead" star still sits dead center of this wreckage suggests that when this explosion happened, it was roughly symmetrical and the kick was relatively light.

"Explosions conserve momentum. If the explosion itself was perfectly symmetric, the ejecta should also be symmetric, and the surviving star should not receive a substantial kick," Caiazzo said. "We were able to measure a precise upper limit of the remnant star's transverse kick speed, showing that it was surprisingly small. We practically don't see the star moving away at all."

While there are still many mysteries surrounding Pa 30 and its zombie occupant, one of the most exciting prospects emerging from this research is the possibility it will help identify the wreckage of other Type Iax supernovas in our galaxy.

"While we can see Type Iax supernovas in distant galaxies as point sources of light, resolving the remnants of such explosions is only possible in our own galaxy and in very few nearby ones," Caiazzo concluded. "Eventually, we plan to use Pa 30's distinctive properties to search the Milky Way and nearby galaxies for similar objects."The team's research was published in The Astrophysical Journal.

Robert Lea is a science journalist in the U.K. whose articles have been published in Physics World, New Scientist, Astronomy Magazine, All About Space, Newsweek and ZME Science. He also writes about science communication for Elsevier and the European Journal of Physics. Rob holds a bachelor of science degree in physics and astronomy from the U.K.’s Open University. Follow him on Twitter @sciencef1rst.

#431. Trump, Putin and Xi at one table, without Ukraine. What Moscow wants from the proposal and why Beijing might agree.

Moscow is pushing for a summit with Donald Trump and Xi Jinping to discuss ending the war in Ukraine, without Kyiv or the EU. What does Russia hope to gain, and why might China be willing to take part?

The administration of US President Donald Trump has stepped up diplomatic efforts this autumn to revive stalled negotiations aimed at ending Russia's war in Ukraine.

Washington has increased pressure on Kyiv and its European partners while seeking to draw Moscow back to the negotiating table through a series of concessions, including a deal involving Russian diesel exports to the US and the temporary easing of some sanctions.

Yet the Kremlin appears in no hurry to resume meaningful peace talks. Moscow is ready to hold negotiations with Ukraine at a technical level next month, the Kremlin said after the meeting between US special envoy Steve Witkoff and Russian President Vladimir Putin in St. Petersburg on October 21.

Putin agreed to resume the peace talks mediated by Washington after November 10, his aide Yury Ushakov said after the meeting. He added that the next round of negotiations could take place in Abu Dhabi and would focus on "building the security system for Russia and Ukraine after the end of the war."

According to his aide, Putin also shared some thoughts with Witkoff on the possible trilateral summit involving US President Donald Trump and Chinese President Xi Jinping. Ushakov pointed out that Beijing is already preparing the meeting.

Moscow proposed to hold a three-way meeting on the sidelines of the Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation (APEC) summit, scheduled for November 18-19 in Shenzhen, China, The Wall Street Journal reported earlier this week. According to WSJ sources, Putin hopes to reach an agreement with Trump and Xi on ending the war in Ukraine on terms favorable to Russia.

Moscow's proposal for the three-way summit involving Russia, the United States and China — but excluding Ukraine and the European Union — offers a glimpse of the negotiating format the Kremlin would prefer. Russia insists that Ukraine must be prepared to make territorial concessions before negotiations can move forward. Putin has also repeatedly stressed that he is willing to negotiate only if Russian interests are taken into account.

According to the WSJ, Putin views the proposed gathering in Shenzhen as a potential new Yalta Conference, referring to the 1945 meeting of the leaders of the United States, Great Britain and the Soviet Union to discuss the defeat of Nazi Germany and the postwar order in Europe.

The newspaper reported that Chinese officials had indicated through diplomatic channels that Beijing would not object to the three-way format if Trump agreed to participate. Trump, whose relations with Kyiv have deteriorated significantly in recent weeks, has also expressed openness to such talks.

Ukraine's President Volodymyr Zelenskyy pointed out during his visit in Oslo on October 20 that talks concerning the future of his country will be acceptable only if Ukraine gets a seat at the negotiating table.

Temur Umarov, a researcher at the Carnegie Russia Eurasia Center in Berlin who specializes in China-Russia relations, is not surprised by the Kremlin's proposal. He told DW that Moscow wants to feel respected by Washington and treated as a major international power.

The previous US administration was unwilling to discuss questions affecting Ukraine's sovereignty with Russia without Kyiv's participation. Now, Umarov said, the Kremlin believes such negotiations may have become possible.

Moscow apparently hopes that any agreement reached at the proposed meeting would prompt the White House to pressure Ukraine and the EU into accepting its terms. But what role would China actually play?

Umarov believes Beijing would participate largely as a symbolic partner, rather than as an active mediator responsible for the outcome. "Beijing has no intention of taking responsibility for Ukraine's future," he said.

Simply attending the summit would not impose any additional obligations on China. "It is hardly surprising that the Kremlin insists on this format," Umarov explained. "Russia wants to demonstrate that it is on an equal footing not only with the United States, but also with China. Yet, objectively speaking, Moscow is not the senior partner in that relationship."

Umarov believes China has little direct interest in such negotiations. Even if the leaders issued a joint statement, Beijing would seek language that was as vague and noncommittal as possible, he said. That would allow China to present almost any subsequent development in the war as consistent with the understanding reached at the summit. For Umarov, such flexibility would be essential to Beijing's participation.

However, Yurii Poita, a senior associate researcher at the Mercator Institute for China Studies (MERICS), offers a different assessment. He believes China and Russia have overlapping strategic interests, particularly in their efforts to promote an alternative security architecture across Eurasia.

Both countries, he argues, would benefit from a reduction in Western military presence in Europe and Asia and from a weakening of NATO's influence. "If Russia and China manage to convince the United States to reduce its military presence in Europe and military assistance to Ukraine, Russia would gain additional advantages on the battlefield," Poita told DW.

He added that such a development would further undermine the EU's confidence in the United States as a NATO ally. Poita believes Moscow and Beijing would seek precisely these outcomes in any negotiations with Washington.

Michael Kimmage, director of the Kennan Institute in the United States, also doubts that Beijing is preparing to take a more active diplomatic role in ending the war.

Speaking to DW, Kimmage said China may see the conflict as destabilizing and would prefer to maintain trade relations with Ukraine and other countries in the region. But he believes another priority outweighs those concerns. "More important to China is the political survival of Vladimir Putin," he said.

According to Kimmage, Russia provides China with several geopolitical advantages, including low-cost energy and a foreign policy partner that rarely stands in Beijing's way.

He believes China will continue indirectly supporting Russia's military efforts to prevent a Russian defeat. An outright Russian victory may not necessarily be Beijing's objective, he said, but ensuring Putin does not lose is crucial. For that reason, Kimmage does not expect China to pressure the Russian president into ending the war if Putin is unwilling to do so.

Neither Ukraine nor the European Union would be part of the proposed talks in China.

Kateryna Krasina, a junior researcher at the Kyiv-based New Geopolitics Network, believes their exclusion could reveal how Beijing actually views their international influence.

In her assessment, China's approach suggests that it does not regard Ukraine as an independent player in the negotiations and considers Europe relatively weak.

Kimmage, however, argues that excluding Europe from the summit would not necessarily diminish its influence over the course of the negotiations.

He acknowledged that European governments may have little power to prevent a meeting between Trump, Putin and Xi. But he pointed to European efforts surrounding the 2025 Alaska summit between Trump and Putin, which he believes helped prevent outcomes that would have been unfavorable to Europe.

Kimmage said Europe's influence has grown through financial assistance to Ukraine and extensive defense industry cooperation, particularly involving Germany and other European countries.

He argued that European support has played an important role in Ukraine's ability to resist Russian advances. "And that support equals leverage," Kimmage said.

He believes Russia, China and the United States may all be interested in portraying Europe as a secondary power, but that such a portrayal would overlook Europe's actual influence.

"The optics and the reality are not quite the same," he said, drawing a distinction between the appearance of great-power diplomacy resembling the Yalta Conference and Europe's role in supporting Ukraine.

He believes that any diplomatic agreement between the US, Russia and China would face the reality on the ground.

"China understands that whatever the outcome of negotiations with the United States and Russia, the reality on the ground will be very different and will depend above all on Ukraine," he said.

"Whether the Kremlin likes it or not, Ukraine has agency of its own. Neither the United States nor Western countries can fully determine what happens on the battlefield through diplomatic pressure alone."

#430. 6 Best Folding Phones (2026): Samsung, Apple, Google, Motorola

You may be scoffing as you read this. The best folding phones? Really? Aren’t those just gimmicks? I'm here to tell you they're pretty darn useful. And yes, there are quite a few now (even Apple is in on the action), so you have options. Folding phones are an evolution of the traditional single-screen slab of glass we've all been using for nearly two decades.

They largely come in two forms: a folding flip phone or a folding book-like phone, enabling you to either relive the glory days of small phones or expand your multitasking options while on the go. A folding phone is a personal decision—arguably even more than a traditional smartphone, since it hinges (heh) on what style you prefer. We have tested almost all of them, and these are our favorites.

Check out our other mobile buying guides, including the Best Android Phones, Best iPhones, Best Cheap Phones, Best Samsung Phones, Best Google Pixel Phones, Best Motorola Phones, Best Phones With a Headphone Jack, and Best Phones You Can't Buy in the US.

Updated October 2026: We've added the Apple iPhone Duo, more on the Z Flip8 after testing, and updated prices.

The Galaxy Z Fold8 features fresh proportions for a form factor that feels just right and purpose-built. It is shorter and wider than the Galaxy Z Folds you might be used to, yet when you open it up, it feels even more tablet-like than ever. The crease is significantly less obvious than previous models, and the Fold8 is lighter and easier to handle than the larger Fold8 Ultra. You lose a little bit of screen real estate, but it's marginal.

The front 5.5-inch screen has a nice 10:16 aspect ratio that doesn't cause ridiculous letterboxing. I also found I opened the inner 7.6-inch screen far more often than with the Ultra, and you can comfortably have three apps side by side in split-screen mode. The Qualcomm Snapdragon 8 Elite Gen 5 chipset ensures the smoothest of rides with apps and games. The Fold8's 4,800-mAh battery (Samsung's first with silicon-carbon battery technology) lasts about five to six hours of screen-on time.

Where the Fold8 loses ground compared to the more expensive Ultra is the camera. The Fold8 has a 50-megapixel main sensor and a 50-megapixel ultrawide, with two 10-megapixel selfie cameras on the outer and inner screens. The Ultra adds a more powerful 200-megapixel main camera and a 10-megapixel telephoto offering 3X optical zoom, making it the better choice for photographers. That said, the Fold8 still takes great photos and will serve just fine for most folks.

If you've been on the fence about foldables, the Fold8 could be the tablet-phone combo to tempt you down. It's pricey at $1,900 (the Ultra is $200 more), but there are already offers popping up, and we'll see more as we head into the holiday season.

The iPhone Duo is astonishingly good for a folding-phone debut, and we're impressed by its polished hardware, graceful hinge, and durability. The outer screen is big enough to be useful, and it opens to a small tablet size with a clever nanotexture finish that almost completely hides the crease. But it is heavy at 254 grams (the Fold8 above only weighs 201 grams).

The side-by-side view for optimized apps on the inner screen is lovely, and Apple has shifted iOS navigation elements to the right, which works well in apps that take advantage of it. As a great choice for multitaskers, you may even consider working on the iPhone Duo. The battery life is excellent, and you’ll likely have around 40 percent left after an average day’s use.

Apple has also added features that take advantage of the form, like Kid Cue to help you catch your kid's attention for photos and Smart Take, which lets you prop it on a table and automatically shoot photos when everyone is posed. It also adds enhanced StandBy support, letting you use the outer screen as an alarm clock while the inside display gently pulses with light to wake you. Apple Pencil support will also arrive later this month.

Familiar folding-phone trade-offs include no Face ID (Touch ID is in the side power button) and no telephoto lens (you get a 48-megapixel primary camera and a 48-megapixel ultrawide). Despite the limitations, this is the first chance for Apple fans to try a folding phone, so even starting at $1,999, it’s sure to be popular.

Flip phones are perfect if you want something that comfortably fits in your pocket or a small clutch. Motorola's Razr Ultra isn't just compact, but it's also the most fashionable smartphone today. You can buy it with suede-like Alcantara or a sustainably sourced wood on the back—both look and feel fantastic. It's also reasonably durable, with an IP48 rating for some dust protection, a titanium-reinforced hinge, and Gorilla Glass Ceramic for more durable displays.

Not much has changed over last year’s Razr Ultra 2025. You get the same Qualcomm Snapdragon 8 Elite chipset, 512 GB of storage, and 16 GB of RAM. The 4-inch OLED screen on the front lets you add multiple widget panels, so you don't need to open the phone as frequently. Once you do, though, you'll enjoy a super-bright, colorful 7-inch AMOLED screen. The big upgrade is the 5,000-mAh silicon-carbon battery, which can easily last two days between charges and is impressively huge for a phone this small.

The 50-megapixel main camera delivers great photos, and there's a reliable 50-megapixel ultrawide and a 50-megapixel selfie camera. While it's a good camera, it still doesn't quite match candybar phones from the likes of Google and Apple. Still, I love being able to take a selfie with the phone closed and see a preview with the front screen, and my wife loves it when I take a picture of her because she can see a preview of herself on the other side of the phone and can correct my poor angle. Video capture is also good, with Dolby Vision support that makes clips look sharp, colorful, and not too grainy in low light.

You'll hear a lot about Motorola's AI features on this phone—there's even a dedicated “AI” button—but the long and short of it is that most of these features aren't very helpful. Pay Attention is the only one I've found myself using due to the nature of my job; it starts a recording with real-time transcription and an AI summary at the end, but most flagship phones can do that these days. Don't buy it for Moto AI, buy it because it's a flipping-cool Razr.

You can get a cheaper folding phone if you're willing to make a few sacrifices. The regular Razr flip phone has a similar design to the Ultra, but with slightly smaller screens. It also makes do with a slightly souped-up MediaTek chipset and less RAM, which means slower performance, but nothing too frustrating. It has the same titanium-reinforced hinge as the Ultra and an IP48 dust- and water-resistance rating.

The camera is still solid, offering pleasing shots in good conditions, but it struggles a little with low light and high-contrast scenes. The outer display is smaller, but still customizable. Battery life is good and should last a couple of days when you need it to.

The fact that you only get three Android upgrades is disappointing, and the Razr has the same unimpressive Motorola AI features as the Ultra. But weighing up the package at this price, the Razr is the most affordable, but still worthwhile, way to try a folding phone.

A polished update to Google’s Pixel line, the Pixel 11 Pro Fold brings several minor improvements. One of the few folding devices with a proper IP68 dust- and water-resistance rating plus the ability to withstand 10 years of folding, the Pixel 11 Pro Fold may be your most durable option. It's also the only Android folding phone with Qi2 25W wireless charging support, meaning you can magnetically stick it to MagSafe or Qi2 wireless chargers.

Even with a slimmed-down body, it's noticeably thicker and heavier than Samsung’s folding options, which makes it more cumbersome to use. Despite a smaller battery than last year's model, it has enough stamina to get through most days, and that's mostly because the new Tensor G6 chipset is more efficient. Google added floating Bubbles for better multitasking, so you can have an expandable chat floating over two other apps in split-screen mode. There are also enhancements for Gemini, including some useful contextual pop-ups in chats to add links to your calendar for events or open destinations in Maps.

Despite the “Pro” in the name, the Fold lacks most of the Pro features available in the cheaper Pixel 11 Pro and Pixel 11 Pro XL. You do get the new HiLight feature, a group of color LEDs in the camera module’s flash that light up different colors based on who is calling or while you chat to Gemini. But you miss out on a much more powerful camera system and features like instant Night Sight, 120X digital zoom, and better video capture. You can still capture excellent photos on the Pixel 11 Pro Fold, but they're not the best that Google offers. That's a bitter pill when you're coughing up $1,900.

Despite the sizable camera bump, the otherwise super-svelte Honor Magic V6 (4.1 mm open, 8.8 mm closed) is a lovely foldable that's easy to handle and feels close to a regular phone when closed. It brings better battery life, faster charging, and greater durability over last year's Magic V5, and also has a versatile camera and a responsive fingerprint sensor in the power button. Honor’s MagicOS is packed with potentially useful foldable and AI features, but also some bloatware.

The biggest enhancement this year is the long-lasting 6,660-mAh silicon-carbon battery, and Honor is promising better durability than before, with an IP68/69 rating for water resistance. There’s also a snazzy new red finish. The Honor Magic V6 is the best book-like foldable for folks in the UK, Europe, or other markets where the phone is sold, though you can always import it into the US. Read more in our Best Phones You Can't Buy in the US guide.

Samsung Galaxy Z Fold8 Ultra for $2,100: The Z Fold8 Ultra is a refined release with a 6.5-inch cover display that opens to an expansive 8-inch screen that feels like two 6.5-inch displays side by side. It's fast and smooth, with a 5,000-mAh battery, but it's the camera that earns it the “Ultra” title. It's an impressive combo, including a 200-megapixel main shooter, 50-megapixel ultrawide, and a 10-megapixel telephoto lens that supports 3X optical zoom. Sadly, it lacks Qi2 support for magnetic wireless charging, and the camera module makes it rock when lying flat on a surface. But the main reason it misses out on a place above is that the smaller Fold8 is so much easier to handle.

Samsung Galaxy Z Flip8 for $1,200: If you want something compact, the Galaxy Z Flip8 is the lightest and thinnest flip phone in the world with a redesigned chassis, and it comes a close second to Motorola's Razr Ultra. It has a 4.1-inch cover screen, and it flips open to become a 6.9-inch candy bar. It's also more durable than ever and boasts the same excellent chipset and performance as Samsung's larger Fold models. The 4,300-mAh battery is solid, but the camera system is relatively basic, comprising a 50-megapixel main shooter backed by a 12-megapixel ultrawide.

Motorola Razr Fold for $1,900: The first book-style foldable from Motorola is good, but not great. The front screen feels very much like a normal phone, and you can open it to enjoy the vast 8.1-inch display inside. Sadly, the Razr Fold lacks the top-end processor of the year, which is disappointing at this price. The highlight is the massive 6,000-mAh battery, which can stretch to nearly three days of use. Thankfully, it does come with seven years of software updates (unlike Motorola's Flip family). Motorola has also added a telephoto lens to the capable, triple 50-MP camera. But, overall, we prefer Google's Pixel 11 Pro Fold.

Motorola Razr+ 2026 for $1,100: The awkward middle child in the Razr lineup lacks the bells and whistles or top-notch specs of the Ultra, but still costs more than $1,000. You can get very close to the same experience from the cheaper Razr and save hundreds. Even battery life is unimpressive, because it has the smallest capacity of the Razr family. The cover glass isn’t the ceramic glass mixture as on the Razr Ultra. I found out the hard way after dropping the phone once, roughly a 4-foot drop onto asphalt, and the front screen shattered. Get a screen protector!

Oppo Find N6 for $1,929: With an 8.12-inch internal display, the Find N6 is a beautifully engineered folding phone that’s impossibly slim and light. Despite its promise of being creaseless, the fold is still discernible. You’ll hardly notice it when the screen is on, though you’ll feel it if you use the optional stylus. Performance is impressively slick, with enough stamina to see you through a busy day, and the camera system is excellent. It’s a big step up from last year’s N5, with a 200-MP main shooter, 50-MP telephoto, and 50-MP ultrawide. The global model can use familiar Google apps, but you’ll have to import it, as this phone won’t officially be released in the West.

Google Pixel 10 Pro Fold for $1,799: The only reason to consider the Pixel 10 Pro Fold right now is if you see it on sale. Google's Pixel 11 Pro Fold is superior, but the 10 Pro Fold isn't far behind this year's release. It's still a reasonably slim device with a large front screen that feels like a normal phone, an excellent 8-inch inner screen, and a triple-camera system that delivers great results, though not as great as the Pixel 10 Pro series. Read our Best Pixel Phones guide for more.

Xiaomi Mix Flip for $899: Xiaomi’s first flip phone has a lovely design with excellent displays inside and out, long battery life with fast charging, and flagship-level performance. It also boasts a solid dual-lens camera, opting for telephoto instead of ultrawide alongside the capable main shooter, which is more useful for most folks. The software lets the party down a little; there’s no IP rating, and it is pricey, but I had fun with this flip phone.

Folding phones are more fragile than normal phones. Folding phones have more parts than a traditional smartphone, not to mention a hinge. Companies advertise how many folds these devices have been tested for—usually around 200,000 or 500,000 folds—but a single drop can be incredibly damaging. They are not as dust-resistant, and some aren't as water-resistant (only the Pixel 11 Pro Fold and iPhone Duo are IP68-rated in the US), so you'll want to be especially careful. Use a case, and it may be worth investing in the manufacturer's device care policy.

Don't remove the built-in screen protector. Most of these folding phones have a film over the inner screen. You might be tempted to remove it, but don't—it can damage the display. The packaging usually includes instructions not to remove it and how to care for your device.

There's a small learning curve to the software. Flip-style folding phones are fairly straightforward, but book-like folding phones that double as tablets have new multitasking tricks that will take some getting used to if you want to make the most of them. I recommend taking some time to run through these features so you understand how they work.

They're expensive. Do you need one? Nobody needs a folding phone. If you think you'll benefit from one, know they're frequently discounted during big sale events like Black Friday, and you can also knock a few hundred dollars off if you trade in an eligible device.

Folding flip phones are for people who want a compact device. Think of a normal phone you can fold in half. That's pretty much what you get, plus a screen on the front to monitor widgets and texts. This does mean you can flip the phone to answer a call and flip it shut to end one—super cool. You can put the phone at a 90-degree angle, and some apps will adapt to show controls on the bottom and important details at the top, like in video calling apps or when watching videos on YouTube, though this depends on the phone. Folding flip phones typically have mediocre battery life, and their cameras aren't as good as their non-folding counterparts.

Folding book-like phones are for people who want the big-screen experience. Are you constantly watching full-length movies on your phone? Or do you always find yourself jumping back and forth between two apps? Then these are for you. They have good battery life and better cameras (though not as good as non-folding phones), but they're far more expensive. These include the short-lived triple-screen folding devices like Samsung's Galaxy Z TriFold. They're thick and heavy, though we're seeing newer models that are surprisingly thin. Still, you may have a hard time fitting them in your pockets.

If you're team Android, this is a good time to buy. Samsung and Google just updated their foldables, and Motorola released its first big foldable in the summer and won't announce the Razr 2027 lineup until the spring. For folks outside the US, we probably won't see new foldables from Honor or Oppo until the spring, too.

If you're considering an iPhone, Apple is likely to unveil a new folding phone soon, so you might want to wait and see what it looks like.

Whichever style you go for, my favorite feature of folding phones is the ability to see the viewfinder on the external screen and snap a selfie with the high-quality rear cameras—just set the phone down slightly folded so it stays upright, and there's usually a button you can click in the camera app to send the preview to the external screen. You can usually trigger the shutter hands-free with a palm gesture. (Motorola's Razr Ultra lets you start and stop video recordings with hand gestures, too!) It's excellent when traveling if you want to take a photo of yourself next to a point of interest.

Many of these phones offer similar functionality with translation—Samsung and Google, for example, let you show your words translated on the external screen so the person you are speaking to can see it, and then they can tap a button on the screen to have the phone start translating their words, which you'll see on the internal screen. It's a much nicer system than using Google Translate on a candy bar phone. —Julian Chokkattu

#429. Comcast beats earnings expectations as media revenue offsets weakness in connectivity

Comcast beat Wall Street's expectations for third-quarter earnings — despite headwinds in its theme parks business — thanks to continued strength in its media unit.

The company's Peacock streaming service reported a quarterly profit for the second time since its launch in 2020, posting $261 million in adjusted earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation and amortization on $1.8 billion in revenue.

The platform continued to reap the benefits of record-high viewership for the FIFA World Cup, as the final games played out at the beginning of the quarter and aired on NBCUniversal's Telemundo and on Peacock.

Revenue in Comcast's media division, which includes the streaming service, rose 13% compared with the third quarter last year to $5.4 billion. The overall content and experiences unit, which also includes its film studio and theme parks, saw revenue rise 6.5%.

Comcast's connectivity and platforms sector, however, continued to face pressure, with revenue down 2.7% year over year.

The company reported a net loss in domestic broadband residential customers of 144,000 and a net loss in its cable customers of 274,000 during the period. Its mobile business added 368,000 customers.

After years of stagnating growth in broadband and heightened competition from wireless providers like Verizon and T-Mobile, Comcast announced in January 2025 that it would refocus on its mobile business and pursue more competitive pricing plans.

"We feel mobile remains a tremendous growth opportunity for us. It's a capital light model, [which] leverages our existing customer broadband base," Steve Croney, CEO of Comcast's connectivity and platforms division, said on an earnings call on Thursday.

Shares of Comcast closed up over 3% on Thursday.

Here's how Comcast performed for the period ended Sept. 30 compared with what analysts expected, according to LSEG:

In June, Comcast announced plans to spin off NBCUniversal and European media business Sky from its core cable, wireless and business services units. The split, which Comcast said would take about a year, will create two separate publicly traded companies.

Comcast co-CEO Mike Cavanagh said on the earnings call that the company is "on track" to complete the transaction by mid-2027.

"While there is still significant work ahead, we have a clear path to completion and remain focused on creating two companies with the strategic flexibility, financial strength, and leadership to compete and grow independently," Cavanagh said. "We have tremendous confidence in the future of both companies."

The split comes after Comcast spun off most of its cable networks, along with an array of digital businesses, into Versant. That separation was completed in January.

For the third quarter, Comcast's revenue was 6% lower than the year-ago period. However, adjusting for the impact of the Versant spin, revenue was essentially flat year over year, Comcast said.

Net income for the period was $3.45 billion, or 97 cents per share, compared with $3.33 billion, or 90 cents per share, in the third quarter of 2025. Adjusting for one-time items, including investments and the value of certain intangible assets, Comcast reported adjusted earnings per share of $1.07.

The company's theme park revenue fell 4.4% during the period to $2.6 billion, which the company attributed to "cyclical headwinds" and lower revenue from admissions, dented by promotional activity. Adjusted EBITDA for the division sank 17%.

The weakness in theme parks comes despite continued investment in the sector with the opening of a new coaster at Universal Studios Hollywood this quarter and Universal's largest theme park property, Epic Universe, last year.

Theme park rival Disney reported record quarterly revenue in its park division in August.

Cavanagh said on the earnings call that in addition to macroeconomic factors, Universal's theme parks struggled due to superior marketing from competitors and technological challenges at Epic Universe. Cavanagh cited new hires, including that of Luc Bondar to the role of chief brand and commercial officer for Universal Destinations and Experiences, as steps the company was taking to address these challenges.

"Looking ahead, we expect performance to strengthen as we move through 2027, with a return to growth in the second half of the year," Cavanagh said. "We remain confident in the underlying strength of the business."

Comcast's studios division reported revenue of $2.97 billion, down 1.1% from the third quarter last year. Adjusted EBITDA for the segment, however, increased 18.6% to $402 million, thanks to lower operating expenses.

The July theatrical release of "The Odyssey," which became Universal's highest-grossing film of all time, boosted the division. Meanwhile, content licensing revenue was down 23.7% from the comparable quarter, leading to the decrease in studio revenue, according to Comcast.

Disclosure: Versant Media Group is the parent company of CNBC.

#428. Scientists solve a 30-year mystery behind inflammatory bowel disease

Scientists from the University of Oxford, Newcastle University, and Cambridge University Hospitals NHS Foundation Trust have uncovered a previously unexplained cause of inflammatory bowel disease (IBD). The discovery could change how doctors understand, diagnose, and treat certain forms of the condition.

The findings indicate that IBD is not simply one disease. Instead, it appears to include several biologically distinct conditions that develop through different processes in the body. Identifying these differences could make it possible to provide treatments tailored to the specific cause of each patient's illness.

In a study published in the New England Journal of Medicine, researchers examined more than 4,900 people with IBD and identified two important findings. First, some patients produce antibodies that interfere with interleukin-10 (IL-10), an essential protein that helps prevent excessive inflammation. Second, this autoimmune reaction explains how one of the strongest known genetic risk factors for IBD contributes to the disease.

Under normal circumstances, IL-10 acts as a chemical messenger between cells, helping the immune system regulate inflammation. However, certain antibodies can block its activity, effectively disabling one of the body's built-in safeguards against excessive immune responses. Without this protection, inflammation can persist and become difficult to control.

Hidden Antibodies Reveal a Cause of Inflammatory Bowel Disease

Inflammatory bowel disease includes Crohn's disease and ulcerative colitis, two chronic conditions that affect approximately 500,000 people in the UK and millions more around the world.

Symptoms often first appear during adolescence or early adulthood, and patients may face lifelong medical challenges. Managing the disease can involve repeated hospital visits, medications that suppress immune activity, and sometimes surgery. Although treatments have improved, many people must try several different therapies without achieving lasting relief. This places a substantial burden on patients and creates significant costs for healthcare systems.

The researchers discovered that approximately 3.5% of patients with IBD had elevated levels of antibodies that neutralize IL-10. These antibodies were detected in people with both Crohn's disease and ulcerative colitis, but not in healthy individuals.

Based on the prevalence of IBD in the UK, the researchers estimate that 15,000 to 20,000 patients nationwide could carry these harmful antibodies.

Scientists Solve a 30-Year Genetic Mystery

The investigation also revealed a strong association between the IL-10-blocking antibodies and a genetic variant called HLA-DRB1*01:03.

Scientists at Oxford first connected this genetic variant to a severe form of inflammatory bowel disease 30 years ago. However, the biological process linking the gene to the condition had remained unclear.

The new results help resolve that longstanding mystery. People who carry HLA-DRB1*01:03 are substantially more likely to develop antibodies that interfere with IL-10, providing an explanation for how this genetic risk factor can contribute to severe intestinal inflammation.

Professor Holm Uhlig, a pediatric gastroenterologist and Director of the Centre for Human Genetics at the University of Oxford's Nuffield Department of Medicine, was a senior author of the study.

"We've suspected an important role of interleukin 10 in patients with inflammatory bowel disease for decades. The study now provides clear evidence and contributes the missing link between a well-known genetic variant that had been linked to severe inflammatory bowel disease in the past and the very recently discovered autoimmunity to interleukin 10."

"Understanding what drives the inflammation, provides a clear explanation for disease in this group of people and opens the door to new treatments that target the autoantibodies themselves or cells that produce those autoantibodies."

How Earlier Discoveries Helped Explain Severe IBD

The findings build on more than a decade of research into rare inherited disorders that disrupt the body's ability to control inflammation.

Sophie Hambleton, Professor of Pediatrics and Immunology at Newcastle University, explained that studying these uncommon conditions provided important clues about the mechanisms involved in more widespread forms of IBD.

"This discovery shows how the study of rare, inherited disorders can shed new light on common conditions. The groundwork was laid over 10 years ago when genetic defects in IL-10 or its receptor were found in young children with severe IBD. Later, we realized that neutralizing autoantibodies against IL-10 were mimicking this effect in 2 little girls with colitis."

These earlier observations helped researchers recognize that antibodies blocking IL-10 could produce effects similar to inherited defects involving the same protein or its receptor. The latest study now establishes the importance of this mechanism in a larger group of patients with inflammatory bowel disease.

A Blood Test Could Lead to More Targeted IBD Treatments

Beyond explaining a previously mysterious cause of intestinal inflammation, the discovery could have important implications for patient care.

Researchers believe the results provide a foundation for developing a blood test that identifies people whose disease is associated with antibodies against IL-10. Such a test could help doctors recognize these patients earlier and select treatments that address the underlying immune malfunction.

Dr. Rainer Doffinger, a clinical immunologist at Cambridge University Hospitals NHS Foundation Trust, highlighted the potential benefits for both patients and healthcare systems.

"By identifying patients early and giving them targeted treatment, we could reduce reliance on expensive ongoing therapy and prevent complications. Even modest reductions in biologic treatment, admissions and surgery could translate into savings of many millions of pounds annually for the NHS, alongside the major benefits for patients through fewer interventions, improved quality of life or even durable remission."

Targeting the antibodies themselves, or the immune cells responsible for producing them, could offer a new treatment strategy for this particular group of patients. The approach could potentially reduce the need for repeated therapies, hospital admissions, and surgery while improving the chances of sustained disease control.

Professor Simon Travis, Professor of Clinical Gastroenterology at the University of Oxford and the Kennedy Institute of Rheumatology, emphasized the significance of identifying a specific biological cause of IBD.

"This is the most exciting discovery in a lifetime specialising in IBD. It means that we can now identify a group where we know what is causing the disease and that creates a real opportunity to change how we manage this disease."

The research marks an important advance toward personalized treatment for inflammatory bowel disease. Rather than relying primarily on symptoms to guide medical decisions, doctors may eventually be able to use biological testing to determine what is driving inflammation in individual patients and choose more appropriate therapies.

The study involved researchers from the Nuffield Department of Medicine at the University of Oxford, Newcastle University's Translational and Clinical Research Institute, and the Department of Immunology at Cambridge University Hospitals NHS Foundation Trust.

Funding was provided through the National Institute for Health and Care Research (NIHR) Biomedical Research Centres in Oxford, Cambridge, and Newcastle.

#425. Dogecoin jumps after Elon Musk replaces Twitter bird with Shiba Inu

Twitter’s traditional bird icon was booted and replaced with an image of a Shiba Inu, an apparent nod to dogecoin, the joke cryptocurrency that CEO Elon Musk is being sued over.

Musk addressed the change Monday afternoon, tweeting, “as promised” above an image of a year-old conversation in which another user suggested that Musk “just buy Twitter” and “change the bird logo to a doge.”

Elon Musk's Twitter promised a purge of blue check marks. Instead he singled out one account

The doge logo appeared on the site two days after Musk asked a judge to throw out a $258 billion racketeering lawsuit accusing him of running a pyramid scheme to support the dogecoin, according to Reuters.

Lawyers for Musk and Tesla called the lawsuit by dogecoin investors a “fanciful work of fiction” over Musk’s “innocuous and often silly tweets.”

It wasn’t clear whether the logo change was permanent. Musk has been known to use Twitter to troll both his fans and his critics.

The price of dogecoin, which is typically volatile, was up more than 20% over the past 24 hours, to about 9 cents. It was trading just under 8 cents Monday morning.

Dogecoin was created December 6, 2013, by a pair of software engineers — as a joke. The name is a nod to the “doge” meme that became popular a decade ago. Its Shiba Inu mascot mimicks that meme: a dog surrounded by a bunch of Comic Sans text in broken English.

#422. Scientists Trapped an Antimatter Particle for Months to Study Its Secrets

Antimatter is not known for being easy to get ahold of. That’s part of why these particles remain so cryptic. But recently, a team of physicists at Northwestern University managed to trap a single antimatter particle for months. Cooling it to temperatures near absolute zero, they could control its quantum states with great precision without even touching it. The experiment, published in Physical Review Letters, represents a breakthrough in our understanding of one of the great mysteries of physics.

In theory, antimatter should be everywhere. Mathematics predicts that every particle of matter has an equivalent antiparticle, a fact that scientists have verified experimentally. The universe should therefore contain similar amounts of both, but almost everything we observe is made up of ordinary matter, and we still don’t know why.

It is possible to produce antimatter particles in laboratories, but studying them is still incredibly difficult. When an antiparticle comes into contact with ordinary matter, the two annihilate each other and release energy. To prevent research facilities from exploding, scientists use vacuum chambers and electromagnetic fields that suspend the antiparticles in isolation. But this only partially solves the problem. Studying these particles, especially experimenting with their behavior on a quantum scale, remains challenging.

For this experiment, researchers at Northwestern trapped a positron, the antimatter counterpart of electrons. The team, led by Gerald Gabrielse, developed a device known as a single-positron quantum cyclotron, which used electric and magnetic fields to hold the positron in a vacuum at a temperature close to absolute zero. This means the antimatter particle was at its lowest energy state, allowing the researchers to induce controlled changes in its quantum behavior. Unlike other methods in particle physics, this approach didn’t destroy the particle. The researchers were therefore able to keep the single antiparticle trapped for months, allowing them to repeat measurements.

So what can taming antimatter particles actually teach us? Scientists know that matter and antimatter have very similar properties. For example, an electron and a positron have exactly the same mass, although their electric charges are opposite. However, questions still remain about exactly how similar these two forms of matter are and whether there are small differences that have gone unnoticed in experiments conducted to date.

To this end, researchers want to compare the magnetic properties of both particles. Physicists have already made very precise measurements with electrons, but achieving the same level of precision with positrons has proven more difficult. According to the American Physical Society, the new device could enable measurements of antimatter particles about 30 times more precise than scientists were capable of before.

So far, the experiment has not revealed any unexpected differences between the two particles. What the researchers have achieved is an improvement in their ability to detect these differences in antimatter particles. If future measurements find that a positron responds to magnetic fields differently than an electron, scientists would have to revise some of the laws they use to explain the behavior of matter and antimatter.

This story originally appeared in WIRED en Español and has been translated from Spanish.
